\section{From a counterexample to a complete explanation: thirteen follow-up packages}
\label{app:explanation-census}

This study followed every dossier in the earlier 285-dossier collection whose annotations described both a failure family and a repair: thirteen in all. The square-root result had already been formalized; for the other twelve, human contributors and coding agents developed formal proofs, followed by separate adversarial model reviews. All thirteen packages contain Lean-checked family and repair statements with explicit assumptions.

Table~\ref{tab:explanation-census} lists all thirteen. Ten of the source dossiers are in the 147-dossier human-approved core; the square-root, array-assignment, and recurrence-sum dossiers are outside it. The packages are mathematical developments of the selected examples, each with its own review record and scope (Appendix~\ref{app:recent-human-review}).

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\small
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.10}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{.19\linewidth}p{.43\linewidth}p{.34\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
Record / topic & What the formal result establishes & Assumptions and scope \\
\midrule
CS 97637/a97647\newline Square root & Failure iff the input is a positive square; strict-guard repair satisfies the specification for every natural input. & Completed before the other twelve packages; proves the corrected algorithm. \\
\midrule
CS 1200/a1206\newline Array assignment & Every $2::3::r$ reaches $2::2::r$; repaired postcondition equals the reachable-state set. & A local program statement, with total correctness under the stated assignment rules. \\
CS 13118/a13122\newline Geometric sum & Correct sum is $(c^n-c)/(c-1)$; exact offset and validity conditions are proved. & $n\ge1$, $c\ne1$ over a field; ordering is a separate real statement. \\
CS 156543\newline Liveness & Every finite prefix has a satisfying extension (applying the standard liveness framework \citep{alpern1985defining}); a later commit exactly characterizes repair at a violated request. & The equivalence keeps the violated request in the fixed prefix. \\
CS 1957/a2388\newline Recurrence sum & Correct sum uses $\sum_{i=1}^{L}i^k$; the asserted equality holds iff $k=0$ or $L\le1$. & Exact powers $n=b^L$, $b>1$, $a>0$, $k,L\in\mathbb N$; explicit $\Theta$ bounds. \\
TCS 2312/a2318\newline Signed cut & Total is $T+a-2(n-2)d$; exact conditions distinguish accounting error, negativity and loss of positivity. & Distinct vertices and additive edges; positivity has a separate condition. \\
Math 1291495\newline Cycle deletion & For a finite connected graph, terminal deletion outcomes are exactly its spanning trees; corrected edge counts recover the target tree theorem. & Delete one cycle edge at a time; the cyclic source premise requires $k\ge2$. \\
Math 1868681\newline Bipartite edges & Maximum is $\lfloor p^2/4\rfloor$; the rational value $p^2/4$ is attained iff $p$ is even. & Distinguishes the exact maximum from the valid upper bound $p^2/4$. \\
Math 782216\newline Handshaking induction & The attempted step holds iff
the graph has an isolated $K_2$ component; it fails for every connected graph with
at least two edges. The zero-edge base case is also
characterized. & Classifies the proposed induction step; the base case depends on the empty-graph convention. \\
Math 994847\newline Adjacency powers & $(I+A)^{n-1}_{ij}\ne0$ iff $i,j$ are reachable; a bipartite obstruction family has certified slack. & The full family includes diagonal entries; the source's off-diagonal assertion requires $n\ge3$. \\
ProofWiki 25056\newline Polynomial content & $\operatorname{cont}(ag)=|a|\operatorname{cont}(g)$, with exact signed-form validity conditions. & Integer form; rational positive content needs a zero-polynomial convention. \\
Reddit 1bq1jtc\newline Prime residues & $6q+2=2(3q+1)$, uniquely in the affine template; the intended prime-residue conclusion is also proved. & Corrects the local factorization and proves the target classification. \\
Reddit 8yjpp7\newline Recursive increment & Repaired expression equals $n+1$ iff $n$ is odd; the recursive program returns $n+1$ for all $n$. & The source branch supplies oddness; the result extends the recorded $4k+1$ family to every odd input. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{All thirteen mathematical follow-ups in the selected explanation study.
The middle column states the formal result, and the last column gives the
assumptions needed to apply it. The artifact index maps each abbreviated
source identifier to its full record ID and selected Lean declarations.}
\label{tab:explanation-census}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Three outcomes.}
The packages illustrate three outcomes, which can overlap. One is a complete classification of failures: the square-root package proves that the original algorithm fails exactly at the positive perfect squares and, separately, that the corrected algorithm is correct on every allowed input. Another is a corrected local statement: in the signed-cut example, at the source-compatible values $n=3$, $T=2$, $d=20$, $f=-1$, and $a=19$, the source's total $T+f$ is $1$ while the correct additive total $T+a-2(n-2)d$ is $-19$. Because the surrounding argument needs a positive total, the package also proves an exact positivity condition (Lean statements \path{repair} and \path{exact_positivity}). The third is a proof of the intended final statement after the faulty step is replaced, which the cycle-deletion, prime-residue, and recursive-increment packages supply. Proving a family or repair statement is not the same as proving the source's conclusion; the middle column of the table says which was done.

\paragraph{Assumptions matter.}
The adjacency-power family includes diagonal matrix entries, while applying it to the source's off-diagonal assertion needs the narrower family with $n\ge3$; in the liveness example, the equivalence fixes a prefix that includes the violated request, and letting the prefix end earlier changes the statement. The saved model reviews record these distinctions next to the formal proofs.

\paragraph{Formal proofs and reproduction.}
\path{mathematics/extensions/index.json} maps the statements to their Lean sources and recorded checks: the twelve later packages are in \path{mathematics/explanations/}, and the square-root proof is in \path{mathematics/extensions/sqrt_boundary/SqrtFailureBoundary.lean} with its repair proof in \path{mathematics/extensions/sqrt_repair/}. Files using only Lean's libraries have recorded checks under Lean 4.30.0 and 4.34.0, and files using Mathlib under Lean 4.30.0 with the pinned Mathlib project; the square-root package also has a separately written second proof and reproduction record, which the index distinguishes from the adversarial model reviews of the other twelve. \path{bugbank/lean/README.md} gives replay instructions.
\end{filecontents*}
\begin{filecontents*}[overwrite]{fc__appendix__structural_case_studies_20260917.tex}
\section{Graph colorings and rainbow Schur triples: statements, provenance, and proofs}
\label{app:structural-case-studies}
\label{sec:counterexample-structure}
This appendix states the two family theorems of Section~\ref{sec:mathematics} in full, records where their witnesses and certificates come from, and proves them. Both witnesses refute published conjectures. They were found on 4 September 2026 by \texttt{gpt-5.6-sol} at maximum reasoning effort, in runs that asked the model to prove or refute the statements without access to BugBank, and were accepted by separate model checks. They entered the research-discovery collection on 10 September, apart from the 285-dossier construction stage and the human-approved core. The Schur witness already supplied an infinite family with exact counts, and the follow-up optimizes the larger two-threshold family it belongs to and measures the loss from moving the thresholds. The graph witness was one cycle blowup, and the follow-up classifies the odd cycle lengths and counts all frozen six-colorings. We developed the family theorems and their proofs with the help of AI tools, and report them separately from the benchmark's dossier and input counts.

\paragraph{Graph certificates.}
Three theorems cover the original witness, the refutation of the source conjecture, and the family. The file \path{mathematics/supporting/sources/C9K2_frozen_coloring.lean} defines the adjacency of $C_9[K_2]$ and explicit five- and six-colorings; its theorem \path{C9K2.frozen_six_colouring_certificate} checks a proper six-coloring in which no vertex can change color, a proper coloring with at most five colors, and a second proper six-coloring, so the first six-coloring is isolated although another proper six-coloring exists. The theorem \texttt{Recol.not\_conjecture1} in \path{mathematics/supporting/sources/PrimeBlowup.lean} supplies what a refutation of the conjecture needs beyond the obstruction: it checks that $C_9$ satisfies the conjecture's hypotheses, using a degeneracy lemma~\citep{cereceda2008connectedness} that covers arbitrarily many colors and shows every induced subgraph of $C_9$ recolorable, and it refutes the conjecture. The theorem \texttt{Recol.cycle\_blowup\_frozen} in \path{mathematics/theorems/CycleBlowupAllN.lean}, which reuses the general lemmas of \texttt{PrimeBlowup.lean} verbatim, establishes Proposition~\ref{prop:cycle-blowup-frozen} for every odd $n\ge5$: the chromatic number, the classification, and the exact number of isolated six-colorings, which gives only a lower bound on the number of recoloring components. The later AllOdd and $Q_3$ certificates have their own formal scopes, stated below; combining those graph statements with the source conjecture's hypotheses is a written argument, not one additional Lean theorem.

\paragraph{Schur certificates.}
The proof in \path{mathematics/supporting/sources/RainbowSchur.lean} counts the rainbow triples of the displayed coloring of $[22m]$ for every positive integer $m$, ordered and normalized by $\binom n2$, and uses the count to contradict the proposed asymptotic bound; this lower bound comes from one explicit family, and optimizing over all colorings is a broader problem. The composed certificate \path{mathematics/theorems/SchurTemplateComposed.lean} contains the two-threshold result of Proposition~\ref{prop:schur-template-optimum} and its connection to the literal coloring; \path{mathematics/builds/schur.json} records the intermediate proofs and compilation evidence.

\paragraph{Reviews and build evidence.}
Each proposition below is proved at its full stated scope by a Lean theorem using Mathlib under Lean~4.30.0. The finite $C_9[K_2]$ certificate has recorded builds under Lean~4.30.0 and 4.34.0 using only \texttt{propext} and \texttt{Quot.sound}. Build evidence for the finite example and the conjecture refutation is indexed in \path{mathematics/supporting/index.json}, and the family theorem's in \path{mathematics/builds/cycle_k2.json}. A second model instance checked each certificate adversarially and found no error; human reviews of the two extensions (C01 and S02) and of later formalizations are reported in Appendix~\ref{app:recent-human-review}. The AllOdd and $Q_3$ certificates were checked by models without a further human-review round.

\newtheorem{structuralproposition}{Proposition}

\subsection{Rainbow Schur triples: improving and optimizing a coloring}

Section~\ref{sec:mathematics} defines ordered Schur triples and rainbow colorings of $[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}$; the colors are red, blue, and green, or $1,2,3$, and integers with the same color form a \emph{color class}.

We count ordered triples, so $(2,3,5)$ and $(3,2,5)$ count separately. The extremal problem asks how large a fraction of these triples a three-coloring can make rainbow as $n$ grows.

In \emph{An Unsure Note on an Un-Schur Problem},
\citet{parczyk2026unschur} give a construction with limiting density
$2/5$ and conjecture, immediately after their Theorem~1, that this is
optimal. Their construction gives all even integers one color and
splits the odd integers into two contiguous color classes.

The recorded counterexample improves that construction by assigning
one color to both an initial and a final block of odd integers. For
$n=22m$, all evens have color~1, odds at most $8m$ or greater than
$20m$ have color~2,
and the remaining odds have color~3. The original refutation proves
that this gives exactly $99m^2+3m$ rainbow ordered Schur triples for
every positive integer $m$, with limiting density $9/22>2/5$. It therefore
disproves the conjectured optimum by improving the constructive lower
bound for the original extremal problem.

A preprint submitted on 16 September 2026 by \citet{hegde2026somewhat} gives the same two-threshold construction and $9/22$ lower bound, together with a global upper bound of $8/15$; our saved model run is dated 4 September 2026. The two dates document the two chronologies, and we leave mathematical priority unassigned. The proposition below concerns the unique optimum and stability within the two-threshold family, not global optimality.

The split odd color class suggests a two-parameter search: move its two
block boundaries and ask which placement works best. The following
proposition turns that search into an exact description of the entire
template. Here a \emph{template} means this fixed rule for assigning
colors, with only the two boundaries allowed to vary.

\begin{structuralproposition}[Optimal Schur thresholds within a template]
\label{prop:schur-template-optimum}
For $0\le a\le b\le1$, color every even integer in $[n]$ color~1,
and each odd integer $k$ color~2 when $k/n\le a$ or $k/n>b$, and
color~3 otherwise. The limiting rainbow ordered-Schur-triple fraction
$D(a,b)$, normalized by $\binom n2$, has unique maximum $9/22$ at
$(a,b)=(4/11,10/11)$. On $a\le1/2\le b$ and $a+b\ge1$, writing
$u=a-4/11$ and $v=b-10/11$ gives
\begin{equation}
 D(a,b)=\frac9{22}-\frac52u^2+2uv-\frac32v^2.
 \label{eq:schur-template-stability}
\end{equation}
The two contiguous odd-block subfamilies, $a=0$ or $b=1$, attain
maximum $2/5$.
\end{structuralproposition}

The public certificate
\path{mathematics/theorems/SchurTemplateComposed.lean} combines the
counting and optimization arguments. Its declarations
\path{template_count} and \path{rational_count} give exact counts for
integer thresholds and for $n=2qm$ with rational thresholds in the
stability region. The declaration \path{paper_limit} proves the limit
for every real $0\le a\le b\le1$ and the literal coloring in the
proposition. At odd $n$, two-sided counting bounds suffice to establish
this limit.

Within the same file, namespace \path{CoPEER.SchurTemplate} contains
\path{unique_global_optimum}, \path{left_boundary_optimum}, and
\path{right_boundary_optimum}. The theorem \path{sharp_stability}
gives
$9/22-D\ge(4-\sqrt5)(u^2+v^2)/2$ in the stated region.
The declarations \path{quadratic_spectral_equality_iff} and
\path{quadratic_spectral_coefficient_optimal} prove that equality holds
exactly when $v=(1+\sqrt5)u/2$ and that the coefficient is best possible
for this quadratic form. Thus the certificate links the finite
colorings to their limiting density and then proves its exact optimum.
The written argument appears in Section~\ref{app:schur-template-proof}.

Every pair of differently colored odd integers supplies two rainbow
triples with one even and one odd addend: their positive difference is
even, and it can appear in either addend position. Two odd addends
contribute only when their sum stays in $[n]$.
Balancing these contributions gives the recorded thresholds: a final
color-2 block of normalized width $1/11$ raises the best template density
by $1/110$, or $2.27\%$ relative to $2/5$. The quadratic identity gives
the exact loss from moving the two thresholds within the stated
region; every nonzero displacement reduces the limiting density.
This explains why separating the odd color class into two blocks
helps, and why the recorded boundaries are optimal within this
template (Figure~\ref{fig:schur-template-structure}).

A better density requires a different coloring rule; finding the best
unrestricted three-coloring remains the broader extremal question.

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=.67\linewidth]{figures/schur_template_structure.pdf}
\caption{The two-threshold parity template has unique optimum $9/22$ at
$(a,b)=(4/11,10/11)$, compared with $2/5$ for a single contiguous odd block.
The analytic proof establishes the optimum throughout the stated domain;
the plot illustrates its dependence on the thresholds (generated using NumPy \citep{harris2020array}).}
\label{fig:schur-template-structure}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Cycle blowups: explaining the failure of recolorability}

The reconfiguration problem asks whether one proper coloring can be changed into another by recoloring one vertex at a time, keeping every intermediate coloring proper. To represent the allowed moves, the graph $R_k(G)$ has one vertex for each proper coloring of $G$ using colors from a set of size $k$, and an edge joins two colorings that differ at one vertex; a coloring may use fewer than $k$ of the available colors.

In these terms a graph is \emph{recolorable} if $R_k(G)$ is connected for every $k\ge\chi(G)+1$, where $\chi(G)$ is the chromatic number, and a \emph{frozen} coloring, one in which no vertex can change color without creating an equally colored adjacent pair, is an isolated vertex of $R_k(G)$.

In \emph{Recoloring via Modular Decomposition}, \citet{belavadi2026recoloring} ask whether recolorability of every induced subgraph of a prime graph guarantees recolorability of all its clique blowups; this is the preservation direction of their Conjecture~1, and the statement matches Section~6 of the originally reviewed arXiv~v2 input. Section~\ref{sec:mathematics} defines prime graphs and clique blowups; $C_n[K_2]$ replaces each cycle vertex by a two-vertex clique, with all edges between neighboring cliques.

The original counterexample uses $C_9$, which is prime and whose
induced subgraphs are all recolorable. Its blowup $C_9[K_2]$ has
chromatic number~5, but repeating the color pairs
$\{1,2\},\{3,4\},\{5,6\}$ around the cycle gives a frozen
six-coloring. Each vertex has neighbors using every alternative
color, so no first recoloring move is possible. A proper five-coloring
provides another vertex of the same reconfiguration graph, proving
that it is disconnected. Thus the blowup fails the conjecture's
conclusion even though the base graph satisfies its hypotheses.

\paragraph{Relation to fall colorings and the source construction.}
A frozen proper coloring is a fall coloring: every vertex sees all colors in its closed neighborhood~\citep{dunbar2000fall}.
For cycles, $3\in\operatorname{Fall}(C_n)$ exactly when $3\mid n$ \citep[Section~4]{lauri2019fall}.
Applying \citet[Theorem~2]{shaebani2009fall} with $G=C_n$, $H=K_2$, and three two-color palettes gives a fall six-coloring of $C_n[K_2]$ whenever $3\mid n$.
Shaebani also explicitly discusses $C_9[K_2]$ immediately after Corollary~3 and gives a fall five-coloring, so even this particular graph predates our recorded witness.
The source's Figure~3 displays the same periodic palettes on a doubled six-cycle; its base graph contains a non-recolorable induced $C_6$, so that example does not contradict Conjecture~1 \citep[Section~6]{belavadi2026recoloring}.
Our case uses this known construction at $n=9$ and verifies all the
hypotheses of the conjecture. In the follow-up, we used AI tools to
prove the odd-cycle characterization and labeled count below in Lean.
The cited constructions establish the existence direction; the
follow-up supplies the necessity and counting arguments. We report these
connections without asserting literature-wide priority for the
characterization or count.

The following proposition identifies exactly which odd-cycle blowups
have frozen six-colorings and counts them, showing that the original example is part of an
infinite family with exponentially many isolated colorings.

\begin{structuralproposition}[Frozen colorings in odd-cycle blowups]
\label{prop:cycle-blowup-frozen}
For every odd $n\ge5$, $C_n$ is prime and every induced subgraph is
recolorable. Its blowup $H_n=C_n[K_2]$ has chromatic number~5 and has
a frozen proper coloring with the six labeled colors $[6]$ if and only
if $3\mid n$. When $3\mid n$, there are exactly $90\cdot2^n$ frozen
labeled colorings, and $R_6(H_n)$ has at least $90\cdot2^n+1$ connected
components.
\end{structuralproposition}

The single $C_9[K_2]$ witness suggests looking at its repeated color
pairs. Call the pair assigned to each replacement clique its
\emph{palette}. The key is that the inability to recolor a vertex
imposes a local constraint on three neighboring palettes. In a frozen
coloring, every three consecutive two-vertex cliques must collectively
use all six colors. Overlapping triples of cliques therefore force
their palettes to repeat with period three. An ordered partition of the six
colors into pairs gives 90 choices; independently assigning each
clique's pair to its two labeled vertices gives $2^n$ choices.
Thus $C_{6r+3}[K_2]$, for every
$r\ge1$, is a counterexample to the same preservation claim. The
original $C_9[K_2]$ has 46,080 isolated six-colorings, not merely the
single exhibited state.

The witness thus becomes a classification: this six-color obstruction
occurs exactly at the odd multiples of three, and its multiplicity is known.
It persists for arbitrarily large prime base graphs while the blowups
keep chromatic number five. Clique substitution creates the obstruction
even though every induced subgraph of the base graph is recolorable.
This identifies what a repaired preservation theorem must address:
additional conditions on the substitution itself. The component count
is a lower bound. At the remaining odd lengths, determining connectivity
requires studying colorings that do admit individual moves.


\paragraph{Larger replacement cliques.}
The two-color palettes lead naturally to a further question: what
happens when each cycle vertex is replaced by a clique of size three?
For every natural $n\ge4$, the graph $C_n[K_3]$ has a frozen proper
eight-coloring exactly when
$8\lceil n/3\rceil\le3n$. This holds precisely for
$n\in\{6,8,9,11,12\}$ and all $n\ge14$.

The necessity argument tracks the cliques containing each color.
Those cliques form an independent dominating set of the base cycle:
no two are adjacent, and every other clique has a neighbor in the set.
For sufficiency, explicit sequences of palettes can be joined to cover
every allowed length. Three consecutive $K_3$ cliques have nine vertex
positions for eight colors, so a color can repeat in the two outer
cliques. This explains why the three-period argument for $K_2$ changes.
At $n=13$, the theorem rules out this particular obstruction; the
connectivity of the other colorings is a separate question.

Allowing the clique size to vary gives a broader construction. For
every odd $n\ge9$, taking $q=\lceil n/3\rceil$ yields a graph
$C_n[K_q]$ with chromatic number $2q+1<n$ and a frozen $n$-coloring.
Thus a suitable clique size removes the divisibility restriction on
the base cycle. We developed these later results with the help of AI
tools. Their graph statements are certified in
\path{mathematics/theorems/Q3Law.lean} and
\path{mathematics/theorems/AllOdd.lean};
\path{mathematics/index.json} records their exact scopes and build
receipts. Combining these graph statements with the source conjecture's
hypotheses is the written mathematical argument, rather than a single
additional Lean theorem linking all parts.

\subsection{Proofs of the structural counterexample propositions}
\label{app:counterexample-structural-proofs}

The rest of this appendix proves the two propositions above. The Schur proof counts rainbow triples, derives their limiting density, and optimizes the threshold pair; the cycle proof derives palette periodicity and counts the frozen colorings.

Both propositions also have Lean proofs under \path{mathematics/theorems/}, with statement and build records in \path{mathematics/index.json}: the Schur certificate connects finite counts to the limiting density and proves the family's unique optimum and sharp stability bound, and the cycle certificate covers every allowed cycle length. Finite checks of the original witnesses are supplied separately under \path{mathematics/discoveries/}.

\subsubsection{Schur template: counting, optimization, and stability}
\label{app:schur-template-proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:schur-template-optimum}]
\textit{Count the two kinds of rainbow triple.}
Fix $0\le a\le b\le1$ and the coloring specified in the proposition.
Write $t=b-a$ for the width of the middle odd block and
$x_+=\max(x,0)$ for the positive part of $x$. The two odd color
classes have cardinalities $(1-t)n/2+O(1)$ and $tn/2+O(1)$.
There are $\binom n2$ ordered triples $x+y=z$ in $[n]$.

For each pair of differently colored odd integers, their positive even
difference yields exactly two mixed-parity rainbow triples, one for
each ordering of the addends. Conversely each mixed-parity rainbow
triple determines this pair. Their number is therefore
$t(1-t)n^2/2+O(n)$, contributing $t(1-t)$ to the limiting normalized
density.

For odd--odd addends, the sum is even and has color~1. Choose the
color-2 addend from $[0,a]\cup[b,1]$ and the color-3 addend from $[a,b]$
in normalized coordinates, subject to their sum being at most~1.
The lattice of odd pairs has density $n^2/4$ per unit area.
The factor two for ordering the addends and division by
$\binom n2\sim n^2/2$ cancel this factor $1/4$. Thus the limiting
contribution is the area of those two rectangles below the line
$x+y=1$. Boundary lattice squares number $O(n)$, giving an $O(n)$
error in the unnormalized count; this also justifies arbitrary real
thresholds and their rounding. Even--even addends cannot be rainbow.

For a rectangle $[u,v]\times[s,t]$, its area below $x+y=1$ is
\[
 \frac12\bigl[(1-u-s)_+^2-(1-v-s)_+^2
             -(1-u-t)_+^2+(1-v-t)_+^2\bigr].
\]
This follows by inclusion--exclusion from the triangle with lower
corner $(u,s)$. Applying the formula to the two rectangles gives
\begin{align}
 D(a,b)={}&(b-a)(1-b+a)\nonumber\\
 &+\frac12\bigl[(1-a)^2-(1-b)^2-(1-2a)_+^2
 -(1-2b)_+^2+2(1-a-b)_+^2\bigr].
 \label{eq:schur-template-objective}
\end{align}
In particular the limit exists.

\textit{Optimize the density over all threshold pairs.}
The allowed pairs form the triangle $0\le a\le b\le1$.
The lines $a=1/2$, $b=1/2$, and $a+b=1$ divide it into four regions. Expanding
Equation~\eqref{eq:schur-template-objective} gives the following
quadratics and Hessian determinants:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{p{0.26\linewidth}p{0.54\linewidth}r}
\toprule
Region & $D(a,b)$ & $\det\nabla^2D$ \\
\midrule
$0\le a\le b\le\tfrac12$
& $-\tfrac32a^2+4ab-\tfrac52b^2-2a+2b$ & $-1$ \\
$a\le\tfrac12\le b$, $a+b\le1$
& $-\tfrac32a^2+4ab-\tfrac12b^2-2a+\tfrac12$ & $-13$ \\
$a\le\tfrac12\le b$, $a+b\ge1$
& $-\tfrac52a^2+2ab-\tfrac32b^2+2b-\tfrac12$ & $11$ \\
$\tfrac12\le a\le b\le1$
& $-\tfrac12a^2+2ab-\tfrac32b^2-2a+2b$ & $-1$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The first, second, and fourth Hessians are indefinite. At an interior
local maximum a twice differentiable function must have negative
semidefinite Hessian, so these three regions attain their maxima on
their boundaries. In the third region the Hessian is
$\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-5&2\\2&-3\end{smallmatrix}\right)$,
which is negative definite. Its gradient vanishes uniquely at
$(a,b)=(4/11,10/11)$, strictly inside the region, with value $9/22$.

The following formulas cover every remaining boundary:
\begin{align*}
 D(a,a)&=0,\\
 D(0,b)&=\begin{cases}2b-\tfrac52b^2,&b\le\tfrac12,\\
                    (1-b^2)/2,&b\ge\tfrac12,\end{cases}\\
 D(a,1)&=\begin{cases}2a-\tfrac52a^2,&a\le\tfrac12,\\
                    (1-a^2)/2,&a\ge\tfrac12,\end{cases}\\
 D(a,\tfrac12)&=\tfrac38-\tfrac32a^2
                  &&(0\le a\le\tfrac12),\\
 D(\tfrac12,b)&=\tfrac38-\tfrac32(b-1)^2
                  &&(\tfrac12\le b\le1),\\
 D(a,1-a)&=3a-6a^2
                  &&(0\le a\le\tfrac12).
\end{align*}
The second and third lines have maximum $2/5$, attained at $b=2/5$
and $a=2/5$, respectively. Each of the final three has maximum $3/8$.
Thus every boundary value is at most $2/5<9/22$, proving the unique
global maximum over this template. The boundaries $a=0$ and $b=1$
are precisely the two contiguous odd-block subfamilies, proving their
claimed optima.

\textit{Measure the loss from moving the thresholds.}
Completing the square in the third region, with
$u=a-4/11$ and $v=b-10/11$, yields
Equation~\eqref{eq:schur-template-stability}. The loss matrix is
$\left(\begin{smallmatrix}5/2&-1\\-1&3/2\end{smallmatrix}\right)$
with eigenvalues $(4\pm\sqrt5)/2$, so in that region
\[
 \frac9{22}-D(a,b)\ge
 \frac{4-\sqrt5}{2}\left((a-\tfrac4{11})^2+(b-\tfrac{10}{11})^2\right).
\]
This inequality quantifies stability within the template: the loss
in density is at least a fixed positive multiple of the squared distance
from the optimal threshold pair. The wider problem allows arbitrary
three-colorings and requires a separate upper bound.
\end{proof}

The public file \path{mathematics/theorems/SchurTemplateComposed.lean}
contains the counting, limit, and optimization arguments. It checks
Equation~\eqref{eq:schur-template-objective}, including every boundary,
using nonnegative polynomial decompositions in the four regions.
For the sharp coefficient, writing $s=(1+\sqrt5)/2$,
$\lambda=(4-\sqrt5)/2$, and
$Q(u,v)=5u^2/2-2uv+3v^2/2$, it verifies
$s[Q(u,v)-\lambda(u^2+v^2)]=(su-v)^2$.
Equality therefore holds exactly on $v=su$. Any nonzero vector on
that line proves that the coefficient cannot be increased for this
quadratic form.

The corresponding declarations are
\path{CoPEER.SchurTemplate.unique_global_optimum},
\path{sharp_stability}, \path{quadratic_spectral_equality_iff}, and
\path{quadratic_spectral_coefficient_optimal}.
The declaration \path{paper_limit} proves the counting and limit
correspondence preceding Equation~\eqref{eq:schur-template-objective};
the composed theorem joins it to the optimization result.
\path{mathematics/builds/schur.json} records the compiled
source and log hashes.

\subsubsection{Odd-cycle blowups: palette periodicity and component counts}
\label{app:cycle-blowup-proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:cycle-blowup-frozen}]
\textit{Verify the conjecture's hypotheses for the base cycle.}
A graph is $d$-degenerate if every nonempty induced subgraph has a
vertex of degree at most $d$. The standard degeneracy recoloring
argument \citep{cereceda2008connectedness} shows that if a finite graph
$X$ is $d$-degenerate, then $R_k(X)$ is connected for every $k\ge d+2$.
Here vertices of $R_k(X)$ are proper maps $V(X)\to[k]$, without
requiring surjectivity, and an edge changes one vertex's color.
To prove the lemma, induct on $|V(X)|$. Remove a vertex $v$ of degree
at most $d$, and connect the restrictions of any two colorings by
induction. Lift that path to $X$. If a neighbor is about to acquire
the current color of $v$, first move $v$ to a different color absent
from its neighborhood; at least two colors are absent because
$k\ge d+2$. Then perform the neighbor's move. Finally give $v$ its
target color. This proves connectivity for all required $k$.

For $n\ge5$, $C_n$ is prime. To see this, a \emph{module} is a set of
vertices that all have the same neighbors outside the set. A proper
module $S$ with at least two vertices would, by connectivity, have an outside vertex
$v$ adjacent to a member of $S$. Modularity makes $v$ adjacent to
every member of $S$, so degree two forces $S=N(v)$. The other
neighbor of either member of $S$ lies outside $S$ and distinguishes
its two members, a contradiction. For odd $n$, $C_n$ has chromatic
number three and degeneracy two, so the lemma proves recolorability
for every $k\ge4$. Each proper induced subgraph is a disjoint union
of paths. With an edge it has chromatic number two and degeneracy
one, so the lemma applies for every $k\ge3$. Nonempty edgeless
graphs recolor one vertex at a time for every $k\ge2$; the empty
graph has one coloring. Thus all induced subgraphs satisfy the full
definition of recolorability.

\textit{Determine the chromatic number of the blowup.}
Call each replacement clique a \emph{bag}, and index the bags of
$H_n=C_n[K_2]$ cyclically by $0,\ldots,n-1$. An independent set takes at most one vertex per
bag, and its occupied bags form an independent set of $C_n$.
Conversely every such set of bags lifts to an independent set.
Therefore $\alpha(H_n)=(n-1)/2$, and for odd $n\ge5$,
\[
 \chi(H_n)\ge\left\lceil\frac{2n}{(n-1)/2}\right\rceil
 =\left\lceil\frac{4n}{n-1}\right\rceil=5.
\]
For the matching upper bound, give the first $n-3$ bags alternating
palettes $\{1,2\},\{3,4\}$. The prefix length is even, so its last
palette is $\{3,4\}$. Give the last three bags, in order,
$\{1,5\},\{2,3\},\{4,5\}$. Adjacent palettes, including the last
and first, are disjoint. Assign each palette's two colors once in
its bag. This is a proper five-coloring for every specified $n$.

\textit{Identify exactly when a coloring is frozen.}
In any proper six-coloring, write $P_i$ for the two-element palette
on bag $i$. Every vertex of $H_n$ has degree five, and its closed
neighborhood consists exactly of the six vertices in bags
$i-1,i,i+1$. It cannot change color precisely when its five
neighbors use all five alternative colors. The coloring is
therefore frozen if and only if, for every $i$, the palettes
$P_{i-1},P_i,P_{i+1}$ are pairwise disjoint and partition $[6]$.
Comparing this partition with that for $i+1$ forces
$P_{i-1}=P_{i+2}$: both are the complement of $P_i\cup P_{i+1}$.
Hence the palettes have period three. If $3\nmid n$, then
$\gcd(3,n)=1$, so this periodicity around the cycle makes all
palettes equal, contradicting adjacent disjointness. If $3\mid n$,
any ordered partition of $[6]$ into three pairs, repeated around
the cycle, supplies a frozen coloring.

\textit{Count the isolated colorings.}
There are $6!/(2!)^3=90$ ordered partitions for bags $0,1,2$.
Each determines all other palettes, and in each labeled bag we can assign the
two colors to its two labeled vertices in two
ways, independently. The exact frozen-coloring count is thus
$90\cdot2^n$. Each is an isolated vertex of $R_6(H_n)$. The explicit
five-coloring, regarded as a six-coloring, has unused color~6, so
any vertex can move to that color. It lies in a further component,
giving at least $90\cdot2^n+1$ components. For odd $n$ divisible by
three, write $n=6r+3$ with $r\ge1$. These graphs refute the same
prime-blowup preservation claim at $6=\chi(H_n)+1$.
\end{proof}

The exact count distinguishes vertex labels and color labels, just as
$R_k$ does. It gives a lower bound on the number of connected components:
each frozen coloring contributes one, and the five-coloring contributes
another. For $3\nmid n$, deciding connectivity requires examining the
components formed by colorings that can make single-vertex moves.
\end{filecontents*}
\begin{filecontents*}[overwrite]{fc__appendix__research_extensions_20260919.tex}
\section{Further certified families}
\label{app:research-extensions}
\label{app:projective-cubes}
\label{app:research-provenance-corrections}
Beyond the three case studies of Section~\ref{sec:mathematics}, further witnesses from the research collection were extended to parameter families and formalized. Table~\ref{tab:certified-research-extensions} summarizes them, and the paragraphs that follow give each result's exact scope, its certificate, and where its witness came from. The prime-blowup and rainbow-Schur families are stated and proved in Appendix~\ref{app:structural-case-studies}. We developed these extensions with the help of AI tools. Separate adversarial model reviews checked their formal statements and their correspondence to the sources. They are mathematical results, not measurements of model discovery. None of the files uses \texttt{sorry} or \texttt{native\_decide}. \path{mathematics/index.json}, \path{mathematics/extensions/index.json}, and \path{mathematics/supporting/index.json} link the statements, sources, hashes, and build records, and \path{mathematics/discoveries/} records the source versions and discovery provenance. Mathematical priority is left unassigned throughout.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering\small
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{.16\linewidth}p{.28\linewidth}p{.31\linewidth}p{.16\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
Case & Refuted statement & What the selected Lean proof establishes & Toolchain \\
\midrule
Projective cubes & Conjecture~4 of \citet{meng2026cubefree} & Cube-free sets above the conjectured density for $d=5,7,8$ in infinitely many cyclic groups; the exact density boundary of the layered construction & core Lean 4.30.0 and 4.34.0; boundary results Lean 4.30.0 with Mathlib \\
Supertoken graphs & $\dim F_2(K_n)=n-1$ \citep{baskoro2025supertoken} & Resolving sets of size at most $n-2$ for every $n\ge8$ & core Lean 4.30.0 and 4.34.0 \\
Binomial polynomials & second assertion of \citet{sun2025polynomials} & No coefficientwise $q$-log-convexity for every integer $m\ge4$ & core Lean 4.30.0 and 4.34.0 \\
Fake-Gaussian positivity & Conjecture~4 of \citet{gatzweilerkrattenthaler2025positivity} & An exact integer polynomial with a negative coefficient & Lean 4.30.0 and 4.34.0 \\
$q$-rious unimodality & unimodality assertion of \citet{warnaarzudilin2025qrious} & A non-unimodal coefficient sequence; the Landau hypothesis certified & Lean 4.30.0 and 4.34.0 \\
Permutation-grid rooks & necessity direction of Conjecture~3.10 of \citet{lewiswon2026permutationgrids} & A placement count congruent to 3 modulo 4 & Lean 4.30.0 and 4.34.0 \\
Toda numerators & Conjecture~7.3 of \citet{labelle2026toda} & A numerator that is not unimodal under any common-exponent regrading & Lean 4.30.0 and 4.34.0 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Further certified families and refutations. Each row's scope, certificate file, provenance, and the human judgments on its dossier are given in the text; the prime-blowup and rainbow-Schur certificates are in Appendix~\ref{app:structural-case-studies}.}
\label{tab:certified-research-extensions}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Projective cubes.}
In the cyclic group $\mathbb Z_N$, the projective cube of a size-$d$ multiset $S=\{a_1,\ldots,a_d\}$ is $\Sigma^*S=\{\sum_{i\in I}a_i:\emptyset\ne I\subseteq[d]\}$, the set of all sums over nonempty index sets. Repeated and zero generators are allowed, and the source prints $\subset$ in its definition but its three-generator example includes $x+y+z$, so we include the full index set. A set is cube-free if it contains no such cube. Conjecture~4 of \citet{meng2026cubefree} states that when $d\mid N$, every projective-$d$-cube-free set $A\subseteq\mathbb Z_N$ satisfies $|A|\le(d-1)N/d$. For each row of Table~\ref{tab:projective-cube-families} and every integer $k\ge1$, let $A_N=\{x\in\mathbb Z_N:x\bmod M\notin F\}$ with $F$ the excluded residues. Reducing a hypothetical cube in $A_N$ modulo $M$ would give a cube that avoids $F$, contradicting the verified obstruction in that smaller group. The corresponding Lean file proves $d\mid N$, that $A_N$ contains no projective $d$-cube, the displayed cardinality, and $d|A_N|>(d-1)N$. Each row therefore refutes Conjecture~4 for infinitely many group orders.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption{Three families of counterexamples verified by Lean. Every row
holds for every integer $k\ge1$. The last column compares the density
of the constructed set with the conjectured upper bound.}
\label{tab:projective-cube-families}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}rrllll@{}}
\toprule
$d$ & $M$ & Excluded residues $F$ & $N$ & $|A_N|$ & Density \\
\midrule
5 & 16 & $\{0,4,12\}$ & $80k$ & $65k$ & $13/16>4/5$ \\
7 & 24 & $\{0,6,18\}$ & $168k$ & $147k$ & $7/8>6/7$ \\
8 & 36 & $\{0,6,12,30\}$ & $72k$ & $64k$ & $8/9>7/8$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The finite obstruction is checked in four steps. A Python generator enumerates the sets of subset sums reachable while all nonempty sums avoid $F$ modulo $M$; these sets describe the possible states after each new generator is added. Lean checks the generated data: appending any residue must either produce a forbidden nonempty subset sum or lead to a listed state at the next depth, and no state survives to depth $d$. A shared soundness proof turns these checks into a theorem about every length-$d$ sequence. Reduction modulo $M$ then gives cube-freeness for every group order divisible by $M$, counting the repeated residue classes gives the cardinality, and $N=\operatorname{lcm}(M,d)k$ supplies the hypothesis $d\mid N$. The three self-contained files compiled under Lean 4.30.0 and 4.34.0 with Lean's bundled libraries and no Mathlib; the batch checks use kernel evaluation and no axioms, the assembled closure theorem uses \texttt{propext}, and the family theorems use \texttt{propext}, \texttt{Classical.choice}, and \texttt{Quot.sound}. The certificates are in \path{mathematics/theorems/}, with build evidence, generator and template hashes in \path{mathematics/builds/cube_families.json}. A separate model reader examined the argument adversarially, regenerated all three files byte for byte, rejected thirteen deliberately incorrect variants, and reran the $d=5$ and $d=7$ files under both Lean versions; for $d=8$ it inspected the recorded compilation logs and recomputed the mathematics independently.

The $d=5$ set was found in the model runs of 10 September 2026, first with BugBank and then with plain prompting. It is the pullback under reduction modulo 16 of the published layered set $C_5$ of \citet{long2019projective}, so the search rediscovered that construction. We found the $d=7$ and $d=8$ sets by computer search, with AI tools, on 19 September and then formalized them; their construction priority is not established. Human reviews of the dimension-5, 7 and 8 dossiers (R02, R13, R14) are reported in Appendix~\ref{app:recent-human-review}. The same source's auxiliary Conjecture~10 had been refuted publicly on 26 July 2026 by \citet{hypnopump2026conj10} with a different witness and a Lean proof; that witness contains a projective cube, so it concerns Conjecture~10, not Conjecture~4.

The three families raise the question in which dimensions the layered construction itself exceeds the conjectured density. Write $C_d\subseteq\mathbb Z_{2^n}$ for the recursively defined layered construction of \citet{long2019projective}; each layer has an index, and the construction fits in the group when all those indices are at most $n$, which $d\le n$ guarantees. Let $g(d)$ be the fraction of the group outside the construction, so that $g(1)=1$ and $g(P+m-1)=(1+g(m))/(2P)$ for $P=2^\ell$, $\ell\ge1$, and $1\le m\le P$. The Lean proofs establish, for every $d\ge2$ and every $n$ for which the layers fit, that $|C_d|/2^n=1-g(d)$ and that
\[
 \frac{|C_d|}{2^n}>\frac{d-1}{d}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 d\notin\{2^\ell-2,\;2^\ell-1,\;2^\ell:\ell\ge1\};
\]
equality holds exactly when $d$ is a power of two, and at the other exceptional dimensions, one or two below a power of two, the density is strictly below the bound. The theorem \texttt{LongWagner.C\_five\_mod\_sixteen} identifies $C_5$ in $\mathbb Z_{16}$ with the certified quotient excluding $\{0,4,12\}$; at $d=7,8$ the layered densities are $27/32<6/7$ and $7/8$, which is why the other constructions were needed there. Cube-freeness of the general $C_d$ is supplied by \citet[Section~3.1 and Claim~3.1]{long2019projective} and enters the conditional Lean theorems as an explicit premise, while the certificates for $d=5,7,8$ verify cube-freeness directly; a general pullback lemma proves that taking a pullback preserves cube-freeness and density. These results are in \path{mathematics/extensions/layered_boundary/}: \path{LayeredBoundaryAllD.lean} contains \texttt{density\_gt\_iff}, \texttt{density\_eq\_iff}, \texttt{card\_C\_iff\_fits}, \texttt{card\_C\_gt\_iff}, and \texttt{card\_C\_eq\_iff}, and \path{PullbackLemma.lean} the pullback lemmas, under Lean 4.30.0 with Mathlib. The original all-dimensions file has a successful execution receipt; intermediate bounds were then appended, leaving the headline classification unchanged, and a replay of the unedited amended bytes on 21~September 2026 exited with status zero and reproduced the logs byte for byte (\path{LayeredBoundaryAllD_RECEIPT.json}). The scoped supplement L01 reviews this all-dimensional extension with the Long--Wagner cube-freeness premise explicit (Appendix~\ref{app:recent-human-review}).

\paragraph{Supertoken graphs.}
A vertex of the supertoken graph $F_k(K_n)$ is a placement of $k$ indistinguishable tokens among $n$ types, allowing several tokens of the same type \citep[Definition~2.1]{hammacksmith2017reducedpowers}, represented by a list of $n$ natural numbers summing to $k$. Adjacent vertices differ by moving one token to another coordinate. The proof in \path{mathematics/extensions/06_supertoken_metric/SupertokenMetricGeneral.lean} starts from this adjacency and derives the shortest-walk distance as half the $\ell_1$ distance, the formula stated by \citet[Proposition~3.1(i), Eq.~(2)]{baskoro2025supertoken}. A resolving set is a set of landmarks whose distance vectors distinguish all vertices~\citep{harary1976metric,slater1975leaves}. The finite witness supplies six landmarks resolving $F_2(K_8)$; padding each landmark with zero coordinates and adding $2e_i$ for each new coordinate gives at most $n-2$ landmarks for every $n\ge8$. The theorem \texttt{conjecture\_4\_2\_fails\_k2} thus refutes the claimed dimension $n-1$ throughout that range, including the claim that it holds for all sufficiently large $n$ at $k=2$. The result is an upper bound at fixed $k=2$; the exact dimensions, and the behavior as $k$ grows with $n$ fixed, are separate questions, and the proof is independent of the separate expensive lower-bound computation for $(8,2)$. We later extended the family and formalized it with AI tools.

\paragraph{Binomial polynomials.}
Coefficientwise $q$-log-convexity requires every coefficient of $V_{n-1,m}V_{n+1,m}-V_{n,m}^2$ to be nonnegative~\citep{liuwang2007logconvexity}, where $V_{n,m}(q)=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom{n+j}{j}^{m}q^j$. The recorded witness has $m=11$ and $n=3$. The coefficient responsible for that failure yields a uniform obstruction: for every $m\ge11$, the coefficient of $q^2$ at $n=3$ is $2\cdot15^m+6^m-2\cdot10^m-16^m<0$, and a kernel-checked calculation at $n=23$ covers the remaining exponents $4\le m\le10$. Together these give \texttt{not\_qlogconvex\_of\_four\_le} in \path{mathematics/extensions/09_sun_qlogconvex/SunQLogConvex.lean}, which refutes the second assertion in the cited arXiv v4 of \citet{sun2025polynomials} for every integer $m\ge4$. The other three assertions, the cases $m=2,3$, and pointwise nonnegativity for real $q>0$ are distinct questions.

\paragraph{Fake-Gaussian positivity.}
For the fixed-$m$ positivity assertion of \citet{gatzweilerkrattenthaler2025positivity}, let
\[
 F(q)=\prod_{i=1}^{21}\left(\frac{1-q^{22+i}}{1-q^i}\right)^{a_i},
 \qquad a_6=a_{21}=1,\quad a_{14}=a_{18}=a_{20}=2,
\]
with all other $a_i=0$. The certificate \path{mathematics/extensions/07_fake_gaussian/FakeGaussian.lean} checks the full numerator--denominator identity and proves that $F$ is the exact integer polynomial represented by its 177 coefficients, with $[q^{43}]F=-1$. The declaration \path{not_conjecture4} refutes the encoded general statement, and a general quotient-uniqueness lemma ensures that every polynomial representative of the quotient has this same negative coefficient. The result concerns Conjecture~4; the symmetric positivity assertion, the source's other conjectures, and eventual positivity as $m$ grows have separate mathematical scope. A model run produced the witness, and we formalized it with AI tools; Appendix~\ref{app:recent-human-review} reports the human review of the dossier (R07) and the scoped supplement G07.

\paragraph{$q$-rious unimodality.}
For the factorial-ratio unimodality assertion of \citet{warnaarzudilin2025qrious}, the recorded tuples are $a=(2,3,5,12)$ and $b=(1,1,1,4,6,9)$. Writing $[j]_q=1+q+\cdots+q^{j-1}$, their quotient satisfies $D(a,b;q)=[10]_q[11]_q(1-q^2+q^4)$, and the full coefficient sequence of $(1+q)D$ is not unimodal. The certificate \path{mathematics/extensions/04_qrious_unimodality/QriousUnimodality.lean} proves the quotient identity and its uniqueness and derives the general negations \path{not_conjecture5} and \path{not_conjecture5Sym}. The Landau hypothesis~\citep[Eq.~(1)]{warnaarzudilin2025qrious} is certified for every general floor profile, including every rational input, and for every structure satisfying the listed floor properties; the written argument identifies the real numbers with such a structure, while the retained Lean proof covers the abstract structure and rational inputs. The refutation concerns unimodality and leaves the separate positivity conjecture as a distinct question. The witness came from a model run; the human review of the dossier (R04) is reported in Appendix~\ref{app:recent-human-review}.

\paragraph{Permutation-grid rooks.}
For the permutation $w=(2,7,4,8,1,6,3,5)$, the Lean kernel computes 155 complete non-attacking rook placements on its permutation crossword grid; since $155\equiv3\pmod4$, this count violates the proposed condition. Using general definitions of permutation grids, words, and complete placements, the theorem \texttt{not\_conjecture310} in \path{mathematics/supporting/sources/RookSpectrum.lean} refutes the necessity direction of Conjecture~3.10 of \citet{lewiswon2026permutationgrids}. Two size ranges matter here: the board has size eight, within the board sizes the source enumerated, whereas the count 155 exceeds the $r\le108$ count range summarized immediately before the conjecture, and the restatement given to the model omitted that source sentence. The certificate establishes this witness and this direction of the conjecture; the converse direction, a corrected spectrum of possible counts, and the smallest possible witness are further questions. The human review of the dossier (R05) is reported in Appendix~\ref{app:recent-human-review}.

\paragraph{Toda numerators.}
A coefficient sequence is unimodal if it never decreases before a peak and never increases after it. The recorded Toda witness of 10 September has numerator $1+q^2$: its coefficients are not unimodal when consecutive powers of $q$ are counted, but become unimodal when the polynomial is written in $q^2$, and under that substitution it is the source's type-$A_2$ example. This suggested a stronger target, a failure that persists when the polynomial is written in any fixed integer power of $q$, a change called common-exponent regrading. While formalizing the result on 19 September with AI tools, we found a type-$B_2$ witness at $\alpha=2\alpha_1+2\alpha_2$ with numerator $1+q+3q^2+2q^3+5q^4+2q^5+3q^6+q^7+q^8$. Its nonzero coefficient of $q$ forces the regrading step to be one, and $3>2<5$ gives an interior dip. The theorem \texttt{Exact.not\_conjecture73\_exact} in \path{mathematics/supporting/sources/TodaNumerator_Exact.lean} formalizes the refutation for general root-system data using the exact defining recurrence after denominators are cleared, checking full polynomial identities. The connection from that recurrence to the rational functions in Definition~1.1 of \citet{labelle2026toda} uses algebra in $\mathbb Q(q)$ and the source's uniqueness result in the written argument. The original model witness and the later, stronger witness retain separate discovery records; the human review of the dossier (T11) is reported in Appendix~\ref{app:recent-human-review}.

\paragraph{Checking definitions beyond the witness.}
A witness can satisfy two definitions that disagree elsewhere. For the $C_2^4$ short-zero-sum record, replacing the last invariant factor by the first in the definition of the exponent, reversing divisibility, or using modulus $2$ throughout still lets the refutation compile, because all of these agree at $[2,2,2,2]$. A companion file gives a separately written, Lean-equivalent formulation and checks true instances at $C_2\oplus C_4$, which the altered definitions fail. These mutation probes~\citep{jia2011mutation} test the definitions beyond the refuting instance; correspondence to the source prose is reviewed separately. The base and companion proofs, with their successful compilation logs, are listed in \path{mathematics/supporting/index.json} as \texttt{ShortZeroSums\_Conjecture.lean} and \texttt{ShortZeroSums\_Conjecture\_Sanity.lean}; the individual mutant logs were not retained.

\paragraph{What was contributed at each stage.}
The research collection contains eleven model-produced refutation cases and one finding already visible within its source. Subsequent checking identified known constructions, including the cube and Schur examples, an index-ratio conjecture that its author had revised before our run, and a semigroup contradiction present in the source's own table. We keep these distinctions so that a reader can see both the mathematical conclusion and what was contributed at each stage: identifying a witness, checking it against the source, extending its range, or formalizing the result. The cases are reported as source-linked mathematical criticism and formal completion, with discovery attribution stated individually.
\end{filecontents*}
\begin{filecontents*}[overwrite]{fc__appendix__verified_counterexample_release.tex}
\subsection{The human-approved core and its documentation}
\label{app:verified-census}
\label{app:retained-core-membership}

The human-approved core contains 147 dossiers drawn from 137 distinct sources, selected from BugBank by the rule of Section~\ref{app:core-selection}; human review is not a condition of membership in BugBank itself. The core contains 147 certificate paths and 141 distinct certificate hashes. After the separate checks of reference-line annotations, it supplies 100 primary benchmark inputs and 131 scorable inputs in total; the 31 additional inputs are shorter source texts. Core membership, review evidence, and benchmark eligibility are recorded for each dossier.

Each dossier connects the source text and quoted claim to a concrete witness, its mathematical explanation, and a Lean certificate. Hashes identify the source packet, witness fields, Lean source, and selected declarations; the saved builds confirm that the selected declarations compile, a separate check lists the axioms used by each selected theorem and confirms that only the allowed foundational axioms occur, and the build receipts identify the compiler environment, dependencies, commands, and logs. Replacement witnesses appear alongside their predecessors, so a correction and its reason are inspectable; Section~\ref{app:source-admissibility} develops two such examples.

\subsubsection{Definitions for describing the core}
\label{app:structure-census}

\emph{Source} identifies where the claim appeared. A \emph{mathematical topic} describes the subject of that claim. A \emph{witness object} describes the counterexample itself, such as a graph, number, function, or algorithm trace. These classifications answer different questions: a graph, for example, can expose an error in an algorithms argument.

An \emph{error mechanism} describes the immediate failed inference: a definition or domain condition, a quantifier, an omitted case, an algebraic or order argument, a count, a structural or analytic inference, or an algorithm invariant. The existing mechanism annotations select the operation closest to the quoted error and retain uncertainty flags.

benchmark inputs. Each panel counts every record once. Source collections
follow the release groups, with the two Reddit groups combined. Error
mechanisms use the saved primary annotations; six dossiers, including

\label{tab:corpus-sources}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Reproduction.}
The repository provides core membership, its inclusion rule, and version evidence in \path{bugbank/core/}. From the repository root, \texttt{python -B bugbank/core/verify.py --release .} reconstructs the core from the saved reviews and checks its scientific content, certificates, and benchmark masks, and \path{benchmark/core/replay.py} reproduces the benchmark scores; the \href{https://github.com/qqliu/CoPEER/blob/main/bugbank/core/README.md}{core README} gives the full commands. Earlier census tables, complete annotation evidence, and coding checks are preserved in \path{bugbank/annotations/}, with portable inspection instructions in its \href{https://github.com/qqliu/CoPEER/blob/main/bugbank/annotations/README.md}{README}.
