\begin{filecontents*}[overwrite]{fc__appendix__cost_repro_procedures.tex}
\section{Instruction study: prompts, grading rubrics, and token accounting}
\label{app:cost-repro-procedures}
This appendix gives the inputs, the exact prompts, the execution settings, the grading rubrics, and the token accounting behind the instruction comparisons of Appendix~\ref{app:complementary-detail} and Table~\ref{tab:cost-repro}. The repository holds the saved inputs, answers, model grades, and usage records in \path{studies/instructions/}, and its \path{README.md} explains how to reproduce every table from them. The prompt quotations reproduce \path{studies/instructions/reproduction/prompts.json}, with only LaTeX escaping and dash formatting changed.

\subsection{Study design}
\label{app:cost-repro-reproduction}

\paragraph{Question and comparisons.}
The study asks how the instruction changes the effort needed to identify a false claim. The initial experiment applied four instructions to 100 proofs with Opus~5 at high effort in the Claude Code execution system; the reproduction ran the same 100 proofs with \texttt{gpt-5.6-sol} and Gemini~3.1~Pro, and a separate set of 60 statements without proofs with all three models, reusing the saved Opus~5 proof answers for its paired comparisons. Instructions are compared within each model, with the token accounting that its execution system provides.

\paragraph{The 100-proof input set.}
Each \emph{proofs100} record contains a problem statement and a numbered proof, stored in the JSON fields \texttt{record\_id}, \texttt{problem}, and \texttt{proof\_numbered}; a manifest fixes the input list and the input and reference files by SHA-256 hashes. These are the earlier instruction-study inputs, not the 100 benchmark inputs drawn from the human-approved core.

\paragraph{The 60-statement input set.}
The \emph{claims60} panel contains 60 statements assessed as false in a skeptical review, each with a reference counterexample. Executable checks support 57 of the references, 47 marked fully verified and 10 with exhaustive-search witnesses; the other three rest on the literature, recorded as two partial checks and one citation-only check in the \texttt{verification\_level} field. Each model receives the statement with its definitions, conventions, and an opaque identifier. The claims and witnesses are separate from BugBank records.

Some definitions hint at a refutation. Before the study ran, we fixed a text rule to identify such hints, the case-insensitive regular expression \texttt{/counterexample\textbar{}claim under test\textbar{}the claim (asserts\textbar{}quantifies)\textbar{}to be refuted\textbar{}refut/i} applied to the definitions field; it flags 13 of the 60 claims. Section~\ref{app:claims60-corrected-accounting} summarizes the comparisons on the remaining 47 no-hint claims and gives the complete sensitivity table.

\paragraph{Arms and prompts.}
Each cell sends one prompt. The proof template is

\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily\raggedright You are assessing a mathematical proof for correctness. Read \{input\_path\} --- it contains the problem statement and a line-numbered proof. Use ONLY that file: no web, no other files, no code execution. (Reading that file with your file-reading tool, or with a single shell command that only prints it, is allowed and is not code execution.)\par \{method\}\par Honesty rules: do not assume the proof is flawed; many proofs are fine. Do not pad. In your structured output set record\_id to exactly \{record\_id\_json\} and arm to exactly "\{arm\}".\end{quote}
with \texttt{\{method\}} one of the instructions below (the arm), \texttt{\{input\_path\}} the record's
input file, and \texttt{\{record\_id\_json\}} and \texttt{\{arm\}} the values the answer must echo.
\noindent\emph{\texttt{refute}}:
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily\raggedright METHOD you must follow --- COUNTEREXAMPLE-FIRST: read the proof looking for its riskiest concrete claims, and try to construct ONE concrete counterexample (explicit numbers, explicit small structure, explicit trace) to some claim the proof actually relies on. The moment you have a counterexample you have checked carefully, report INVALID with that counterexample and STOP --- do not audit the rest of the proof. If after honest effort you cannot refute anything and believe the proof is correct, report VALID with counterexample "".\end{quote}
\noindent\emph{\texttt{verify\_full}}:
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily\raggedright METHOD you must follow --- COMPLETE VERIFICATION: verify the proof step by step. For EVERY numbered step, decide whether it is justified given the problem and the preceding steps, and record a one-line justification or objection per step in assessment\_summary. You must judge every step even after finding a problem. Verdict INVALID if any step fails (name the first failing step in flawed\_step), VALID otherwise. counterexample may be "" --- your job is verification, not witness construction.\end{quote}
\noindent\emph{\texttt{verify\_earlystop}}:
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily\raggedright METHOD you must follow --- VERIFICATION WITH EARLY STOP: verify the proof step by step in order. For each numbered step, decide whether it is justified given the problem and the preceding steps. You MAY stop at the first step you are certain fails; report INVALID with that step in flawed\_step. If you reach the end, report VALID. counterexample may be "" --- your job is verification, not witness construction.\end{quote}
\noindent\emph{\texttt{verify\_earlystop\_witness}}:
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily\raggedright METHOD you must follow --- VERIFICATION WITH EARLY STOP, WITNESS REQUIRED: verify the proof step by step in order. For each numbered step, decide whether it is justified given the problem and the preceding steps. You MAY stop at the first step you are certain fails --- but a verdict of INVALID is only complete when you ALSO produce a CONCRETE counterexample (explicit numbers, explicit small structure, explicit trace) to that failing step, checked carefully, in the counterexample field. This arm exists to equalize deliverables: your INVALID must come with the same artifact a counterexample-first assessment produces. If you reach the end without a definitive failure, report VALID with counterexample "".\end{quote}
For the claims the template reads
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily\raggedright You are assessing a mathematical statement for truth. Read \{input\_path\} --- it contains the statement and the definitions and conventions under which it is to be read. Use ONLY that file: no web, no other files, no code execution. (Reading that file with your file-reading tool, or with a single shell command that only prints it, is allowed and is not code execution.)\par \{method\}\par Honesty rules: do not assume the statement is false; many statements are true. Do not pad. In your structured output set record\_id to exactly \{record\_id\_json\} and arm to exactly "\{arm\}".\end{quote}
and the verification instruction becomes a complete proof attempt; the counterexample-first instruction is
unchanged in method:
\noindent\emph{\texttt{refute}}:
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily\raggedright METHOD you must follow --- COUNTEREXAMPLE-FIRST: read the statement looking for its riskiest implication, and try to construct ONE concrete counterexample (explicit numbers, explicit small structure, explicit trace) that satisfies every hypothesis of the statement and violates its conclusion. The moment you have a counterexample you have checked carefully, report INVALID with that counterexample and STOP --- do not attempt a proof. If after honest effort you cannot refute it and believe the statement is true, report VALID with counterexample "".\end{quote}
\noindent\emph{\texttt{prove\_full}}:
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily\raggedright METHOD you must follow --- COMPLETE PROOF ATTEMPT: attempt to prove the statement. Write a complete numbered step-by-step argument in assessment\_summary and, for EVERY numbered step, decide whether it is justified given the definitions and the preceding steps, recording a one-line justification or objection per step. You must judge every step even after finding a problem. Verdict VALID if every step is justified and the argument proves the statement; INVALID if the argument fails and you conclude the statement is false (name the first failing step in flawed\_step). counterexample may be "" --- your job is verification by proof, not witness construction.\end{quote}
\noindent\emph{\texttt{prove\_earlystop}}:
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily\raggedright METHOD you must follow --- PROOF ATTEMPT WITH EARLY STOP: attempt to prove the statement step by step in order. For each numbered step, decide whether it is justified given the definitions and the preceding steps. You MAY stop at the first step you are certain cannot be justified; report INVALID with that step in flawed\_step. If your argument completes, report VALID. counterexample may be "" --- your job is verification by proof, not witness construction.\end{quote}
\noindent\emph{\texttt{prove\_earlystop\_witness}}:
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily\raggedright METHOD you must follow --- PROOF ATTEMPT WITH EARLY STOP, WITNESS REQUIRED: attempt to prove the statement step by step in order. For each numbered step, decide whether it is justified given the definitions and the preceding steps. You MAY stop at the first step you are certain cannot be justified --- but a verdict of INVALID is only complete when you ALSO produce a CONCRETE counterexample (explicit numbers, explicit small structure, explicit trace) to the statement, checked carefully, in the counterexample field. This arm exists to equalize deliverables: your INVALID must come with the same artifact a counterexample-first assessment produces. If your argument completes without a definitive failure, report VALID with counterexample "".\end{quote}
Within each input set the four instructions share the surrounding prompt, the output schema, the grading rubric, and the grader; only the method block changes. The shared proof text has 338 characters before the method and 179 after it, and the statement text 367 and 186. Each condition receives one input file and no retrieved BugBank records.

The answer schema requires six string fields, \texttt{record\_id}, \texttt{arm}, \texttt{verdict}, \texttt{flawed\_step}, \texttt{counterexample}, and \texttt{assessment\_summary}, with the verdict one of \texttt{VALID}, \texttt{INVALID}, or \texttt{CANNOT\_DECIDE} and no extra fields. The initial study called the last field \texttt{reasoning\_summary}; the Opus~5 interface rejected that name during the reproduction, so all models used \texttt{assessment\_summary}.

\paragraph{Execution setup.}
Each model--input--instruction combination runs as one command-line session under Slurm (\url{https://slurm.schedmd.com/}) in a bubblewrap sandbox (\url{https://github.com/containers/bubblewrap}). The sandbox hides the research repository, scratch and temporary directories, and previous sessions. Within the session, it exposes the working directory, the answer schema, and one input file, with credentials supplied separately, while the rest of the host file system and the network remain visible. The prompt permits only reading the input, either with a file-reading tool or with a shell command that prints the file.

The configurations are:
\begin{itemize}
\item Opus~5: \texttt{claude -p}, high effort, only the \texttt{Read} tool,
with the CLI's JSON-schema output option.
\item GPT-5.6 Sol: \texttt{codex exec}, codex-cli 0.154.0, high reasoning
effort, approvals disabled, read-only sandbox, and the CLI's output schema.
\item Gemini~3.1~Pro: the High variant in Google Antigravity CLI 1.2.3,
high effort, JSON-schema output, and a 55-minute print timeout.
\end{itemize}

Codex and Antigravity retain tools that their interfaces do not let the driver remove, so a rule fixed before the runs flags any Codex shell command other than printing the input and any Antigravity tool step other than viewing the input or finishing. Such calls occur in one to three Codex cells per proof instruction, two to six Gemini cells per proof instruction, and up to three Gemini cells per statement instruction; their effect on the statement comparisons is reported below. Answers returned without reading the input are rejected and retried.

\paragraph{Retries and recovery.}
The driver runs six concurrent sessions for each Sol run and four for the Opus and Gemini runs and for each grading job, with a 3{,}600-second limit per session. Transport failures, timeouts, refusals, and invalid answer formats allow up to three attempts. A rate- or usage-limit signal (a structured signal from Claude, or matching CLI error messages from Codex and Antigravity) triggers a 20-minute pause without consuming an attempt. Six consecutive failures trigger a separate 20-minute pause, and three such pauses stop the driver. A cell that exhausts its attempts remains without an answer. Jobs relaunched after the recorded Codex interruption received an enlarged limit of six attempts.

The ledger records every attempt with its prompt hash. Slurm job identifiers in the lock files allow an interrupted run to resume while keeping its completed answers. The driver can also recover an answer from the newest usage record if recording was interrupted, a fallback that no reported run needed.

\paragraph{Token accounting.}
The main ratios use the output tokens of the accepted attempt: Claude's per-model \texttt{outputTokens}, Codex's \texttt{turn.completed} output, and Gemini's result usage, each including the engine's reported reasoning. Uncached and cached input come from the same receipts. Additional attempts, and attempts whose usage could not be recovered, are reported separately; when an interrupted shell failed to save a usage file, the available usage was recovered from the event log.

In the initial Opus study the agents also wrote their answers through a tool call, which added that text to output usage; the reproduction emits the structured answer once. This changes the absolute count by roughly one answer per cell in every condition and leaves the paired design intact.

\subsection{Token accounting for the 60-statement panel}
\label{app:claims60-corrected-accounting}

\begin{table}[t]
\centering\small
\input{fc__generated__claims60_main_with_intervals}
\caption{The separate 60-statement panel. S is search, O an ordinary early-stop proof attempt, and W a proof attempt that must supply a counterexample. Confirmed-objection counts use the same reference-assisted grader. Ratios compare accepted-attempt token sums. Recorded total tokens are uncached input, cached input, and output (with reported reasoning counted once within output), including reported Opus auxiliary usage. Brackets are pointwise paired 95\% intervals; output uses 2,000 resamples and recorded totals 20,000.}
\label{tab:claims60-evidence}
\end{table}


The accounting joins all 720 accepted usage records to their native receipts and completed ledger attempts, and every answer and grade to its record and instruction; all 120 input and reference files match the source manifest. Recomputing usage leaves the saved answers, grades, and output-token counts unchanged. The complete component table for all four instructions (\path{claims60_corrected_components.tex}), the full-panel comparisons (\path{RESULTS.json} and \path{README.md}), and the 3,018 source-file hashes are in \path{studies/instructions/corrected_claims60_accounting/}, with portable replay instructions in \path{studies/instructions/README.md}; the complete subset-sensitivity table is Table~\ref{tab:claims60-all-sensitivities} below.

\paragraph{Counting each component once.}
Sol's native input count already includes cached reads, whereas Gemini's \texttt{input\_tokens} holds only uncached input, so its cached reads must be added; an earlier Gemini summary had subtracted them. Adding the disjoint components gives search/witness totals of 2,480,272/2,745,952 and a ratio of $0.903$, correcting $0.872$, and changes the search/full-proof ratio from $0.978$ to $1.031$; output ratios are unchanged. Opus receipts report uncached input, cache creation, cache reads, and output separately. Reasoning is counted once, within output, for every model.


Opus~5 reports usage for the target model and for auxiliary models. Its target-model search/witness totals are 751,620/895,394, a ratio of $0.839$; including the reported auxiliary usage gives 824,253/970,902 and $0.849$, the all-reported total that Table~\ref{tab:claims60-evidence} uses, while the saved accounting also keeps the target-model view. Sol and Gemini supply native aggregate totals without an auxiliary-model breakdown.


\paragraph{Do hints or additional executions change the pattern?}
Two observational subsets check the pattern: the no-hint subset removes the 13 claims flagged by the pre-run text rule, and the no-additional-execution subset keeps a task pair only when both of its runs recorded no extra execution. Neither subset randomizes tool permissions, and Opus has no execution-count field. For Gemini, excluding the pairs with additional executions changes the output and the all-reported total-token ratios as follows. Against a full proof attempt (59 pairs), the output ratio moves from $0.858$ to $0.844$ and the total-token ratio from $1.031$ to $0.968$. Against ordinary early stopping (59 pairs), they move from $1.275$ to $1.352$ and from $1.210$ to $1.046$. Against early stopping with a counterexample required (57 pairs), they move from $0.739$ to $0.654$ and from $0.903$ to $0.923$. Sol has no such pairs.

On the 47 no-hint claims, the search/witness output ratios are $0.535$, $0.689$, and $0.396$ for Sol, Gemini, and Opus, and the corresponding all-reported total-token ratios are $0.951$, $0.942$, and $0.809$: lower output-token counts can coexist with similar recorded totals. Table~\ref{tab:claims60-all-sensitivities} gives all three comparisons across all subsets, including the extra output used by search relative to ordinary early stopping.

\begingroup\scriptsize\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\input{fc__generated__claims60_complete_sensitivity}
\endgroup



\paragraph{Additional attempts.}
The solver ledgers contain 730 attempts: Sol has no rejected attempt, Gemini has three with known usage totaling 29,135 tokens, and Opus has six failed attempts with unknown usage and one rate-limited attempt with known target-model usage of 283,653 tokens. Unknown usage is recorded as unknown, including when a receipt lists zero API messages. The accepted-attempt ratios therefore describe completed solver answers; grading, later checking, and unrecorded interrupted attempts need separate accounting before an end-to-end cost can be estimated.

\paragraph{Grading.}
The grader, \texttt{gpt-6-astra} through Codex CLI at maximum effort, grades the new answers and regrades the 400 original Opus proof answers, receiving the assessment and the held-out reference inline in the same read-only sandbox with its shell tool available. The recorded September~15 pause contains 1,700 completed grading cells, six with shell commands including three attempted arXiv downloads; the completed dataset contains 1,920 cells, seven with shell commands. The extra 220 completions and the additional command-bearing cell are documented in \path{studies/instructions/reproduction/grader_cohort.json}, with usage and recorded commands in its adjacent \path{grade_usage/} directory; the 1,700-cell pause cohort was reconstructed from timestamps.

The two saved rubrics follow; their judgments are weighed against the source and witness checks in Section~\ref{app:reference-reconciliation}. The proof rubric is:

\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily\raggedright You are grading one proof assessment against held-out ground truth.\par Read these two files:\\ 1. \{assessment\_path\} - the assessment (verdict, claimed flawed step, counterexample). If this file does not exist, use grade MISSED with justification "assessment absent (quota interruption)".\\ 2. \{truth\_path\} - the ground truth: the confirmed flaw and its independently verified witness.\par Every proof in this sample IS flawed (pipeline-confirmed). Grade the assessment:\\ - CAUGHT\_MATCHING: it reported INVALID and its flawed step/counterexample targets the SAME defect as the ground truth (rephrasing and a different-but-valid witness for the same claim both count as matching).\\ - CAUGHT\_DIFFERENT: it reported INVALID with a DIFFERENT flaw, and after checking its support concretely yourself, that flaw is genuine.\\ - MISSED: it reported VALID or CANNOT\_DECIDE.\\ - FALSE\_CATCH: it reported INVALID but the stated flaw does not hold up under your concrete check (wrong math, misread claim, invalid witness).\\ Be strict about FALSE\_CATCH vs CAUGHT\_DIFFERENT: verify, do not take the assessment's word.\par Set record\_id exactly \{record\_id\_json\}, arm "\{arm\}".\end{quote}
The statement rubric adds two outcomes, a correct verdict without a checkable refutation and an object the grader cannot verify; both input sets share this grade list, and in Sol's early-stop proof condition one answer received each added grade, counted separately from confirmed and unsupported objections. The exact statement rubric is:
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily\raggedright You are grading one assessment of a mathematical statement against held-out ground truth.\par Read these two files:\\ 1. \{assessment\_path\} - the assessment (verdict VALID = the statement is true, INVALID = false, CANNOT\_DECIDE; a claimed failing step; a counterexample).\\ 2. \{truth\_path\} - the ground truth: the statement is FALSE and this file gives an independently verified counterexample (the reference witness) and, where recorded, why the statement fails.\par Every statement in this sample IS false (reviewer-confirmed, witness verified). Grade the assessment:\\ - CAUGHT\_MATCHING: it reported INVALID and its counterexample (or, if it gave no explicit witness, its stated reason) is a VALID refutation of the same kind as the reference (the same object or family, or the same failing mechanism; a different-but-valid witness of the same kind counts as matching).\\ - CAUGHT\_DIFFERENT: it reported INVALID with a DIFFERENT valid refutation (a genuinely different counterexample object or a different correct reason), and after checking its support concretely yourself, that refutation is genuine.\\ - MISSED: it reported VALID or CANNOT\_DECIDE.\\ - FALSE\_CATCH: it reported INVALID but the stated counterexample or reason does not hold up under your concrete check (wrong math, an object that violates a hypothesis or satisfies the conclusion, a misread statement, or no checkable support at all).\\ - CAUGHT\_UNSUPPORTED: it reported INVALID (the correct verdict) but gave neither a counterexample nor a self-contained argument that the statement is false. A 'stated reason' counts as a refutation only if it is itself a complete argument (for example a parity or counting argument, or a named object together with why it meets every hypothesis and fails the conclusion). 'My proof attempt fails at step k', 'the hypotheses do not force the conclusion', or naming a gap in a would-be proof is NOT a refutation.\\ - UNVERIFIED: it reported INVALID with a named or large object (a famous graph, a structure with dozens of vertices, a construction you cannot check by hand from the assessment's own text) that is NOT the reference object and that you cannot verify concretely. Never guess: use UNVERIFIED rather than CAUGHT\_DIFFERENT or FALSE\_CATCH in that case. If the object IS the reference object (or an explicitly stated variant the truth file already covers), grade CAUGHT\_MATCHING.\\ Be strict about FALSE\_CATCH vs CAUGHT\_*: verify concretely, do not take the assessment's word; a counterexample must satisfy every hypothesis in the definitions and violate the conclusion. FALSE\_CATCH is only for INVALID whose supplied witness or argument is wrong under your concrete check. Clauses in the definitions that describe what a counterexample must include are guidance to the solver: grade on whether the object refutes the statement under the stated conventions, performing any omitted verification (trace, tie-break check, non-isomorphism) yourself; omission of demanded verification text alone never makes a FALSE\_CATCH.\par Set record\_id exactly \{record\_id\_json\}, arm "\{arm\}".\end{quote}
\end{filecontents*}

\section{The localization benchmark: protocol and complete results}
\label{app:certified-localization}

\paragraph{Current evaluation set.}
The main evaluation uses 100 texts from the human-approved core, each with at least ten nonblank source lines, grouped into 92 problem clusters; a supplementary evaluation adds 31 shorter texts, for 131 scorable inputs in 123 clusters. The remaining core dossiers are 12 pilot records, one unscorable record, and three records with unresolved reference-line concerns; the line concerns affect which lines to score, and those dossiers remain in the core. Appendix~\ref{app:recent-human-review} describes the reviews, the core's selection, and every exclusion.

The current scores reuse the saved model answers under the original scoring rules; membership was fixed from the review evidence before the answers were rescored. The inclusion ledger is \path{bugbank/core/membership.json}, and \path{benchmark/core/README.md} explains how to reproduce both evaluation sets.

\paragraph{Inputs and reference lines.}
A critic receives the numbered source text and a request for up to three candidate lines, ranked by preference, with a brief explanation. The prompt says that the text contains an error and withholds the dossier's selected passage, counterexample, and Lean certificate. For some arXiv records the supplied text is an excerpt from the paper; the released input is in every case the text the critic was asked to assess.

The reference lines of an input are its accepted error locations, including restatements and instantiations of the challenged claim; selecting any of them earns credit, and 57 of the 100 primary inputs have more than one. Model-assisted review checked and expanded these sets. Of the 100 primary inputs, 69 have a recorded positive human check of the current reference-line selection, 55 of them on the exact current source text and labels; dossier approval and reference-line review are separate checks (Appendix~\ref{app:recent-human-review}).

\paragraph{Scoring and uncertainty.}
Hit@1 counts a match in the first candidate slot, and Hit@3 a match in any of the original first three slots. An invalid, unknown, blank-line, or duplicate label leaves its slot empty, and a later candidate never moves forward to fill it. Missing answers and attempts that break the execution rules or exhaust their budget count as misses. We report these all-input rates together with the number of responses that met the rules; rates conditional on those responses have denominators that differ across configurations.

Four deterministic rules choose the longest line, the line with the most words, the line with the most mathematical symbols, or the line most similar to the document's TF--IDF word profile; the comparison baseline is the strongest of the four on the evaluated population, and random line selection is reported as its exact expectation.

Confidence intervals use 10,000 bootstrap resamples of problem clusters, keeping related texts together within the assignment groups specified in the released scorer~\citep{efron1979bootstrap,field2007clustered}; the comparison with the strongest rule reselects that rule in each resample. These intervals describe variation across the evaluated texts. The saved parser, scoring code, grouping, and seed settings are in \path{benchmark/code/}.

\paragraph{Saved responses and reproduction.}
The release preserves the selected responses, failure categories, and every execution attempt; recorded tokens, lower bounds for incomplete logging, and budget charges are kept as separate quantities. The settings below explain how an attempt is run, and \path{benchmark/core/README.md} reproduces the current scores from the saved responses.

\input{fc__appendix__clear_ccl_complete_table}

GPT-OSS 120B & 131,072 & $T=0.6$, $p=0.95$; medium & Local vLLM\\
GPT-OSS 20B & 131,072 & $T=0.6$, $p=0.95$; medium & Local vLLM\\
Kimi Linear & 32,768 & $T=0.6$, $p=0.95$ & Local vLLM\\
Nemotron 3.5 Lightning & 131,072 & $T=1.0$, $p=0.95$; thinking & Local vLLM\\
Nemotron 3 Super & 65,536 & $T=1.0$, $p=0.95$; thinking & Local vLLM\\
Gemini 3.1 Pro & n.r. & Low effort & agy 1.2.8\\
GPT-5.6 Luna & n.r. & Medium effort & Codex CLI 0.155.1\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Saved execution settings for the 13 model configurations. Every attempt has a 16,000-token output allowance and a 600-second wall limit, with the interface-specific enforcement described below. $T$ is temperature and $p$ is top-$p$; local generation uses vLLM 0.29.0 and seed 20260921. ``n.r.'' means the hosted CLI's numeric context-window limit was not recorded. Context limits, output-budget enforcement and recovery rules are specified below. Available version receipts agree across the initial, correction and recovery passes.}
\label{tab:ccl-execution-settings}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Prompts and context.}
All configurations receive the same numbered text and benchmark instructions. Local models use their native chat templates, GPT-OSS places the instructions in the Harmony developer block, and Gemini places them at the start of the user turn. For local models, the prompt plus the 16,000-token output allowance must fit both the configured context and the 120,000-token attempt ceiling; longer inputs receive a recorded setup refusal and a score of zero, without truncation or a model call. The hosted runs preserve the CLI settings and receipts, whose saved configuration does not specify a numeric context-window limit.

\paragraph{Output and time limits.}
Each attempt has a 600-second wall limit and a 120,000-token attributable-usage ceiling, and the common 16,000-token output allowance is enforced differently across interfaces. Local vLLM generation sets \texttt{max\_tokens=16000}, including generated reasoning tokens, and reaching that limit counts as budget exhaustion. Claude requests a 16,000-token client limit and checks the event stream for continuations, output usage, and live estimates. Gemini and Luna have no provider-side output cap in these saved interfaces: their recorded native output usage is checked after generation, and a completed response above the allowance counts as budget exhaustion, with Gemini's output count including its recorded thinking tokens. The 120,000-token ceiling is checked during the Claude and Gemini streams and after receipt of Luna's native usage. Because of these interface differences, the shared allowances do not imply matched computation.

\paragraph{Tools, retries and recovery.}
Claude and Luna disable tools, and local generation has no tool executor. Gemini's CLI exposes tools, so the runner stops and rejects an attempt if a tool step or an additional turn appears. Ordinary runs and the input-correction pass allow one initial attempt and one retry for an eligible transport failure; responses that exhaust their budget or break the execution rules are terminal under that allowance. The separate Haiku continuation resumes the listed no-answer session-limit notices and carries forward completed answers and budget failures. The later environment-recovery pass grants one attempt per listed target, with no automatic retry, and its result replaces the notice regardless of outcome. Both Haiku reporting views use the same corrected and recovered outputs, and every attempt remains in campaign accounting.

\paragraph{Usage and budget charges.}
Recorded usage, accounting charges, and execution limits are distinct quantities. Complete native usage contributes its measured count, partial native usage contributes its recorded lower bound, and an attempt with incomplete usage receives a conservative accounting charge of at least 40,000 tokens, or a larger recorded lower bound or applicable reservation. A charge is a budget debit, not measured consumption. The results table reports selected-response usage; \path{benchmark/accounting/README.md} gives the full accounting rules and attempt ledger, and \path{benchmark/code/generation/README.md} documents the generation drivers, prompts, and settings.

\subsection{Reference-line checks and review exclusions}
\label{app:ccl_detailed_sensitivities}

Human review checked whether the accepted lines state the challenged claim. The initial 40-input check approved 35 line selections; after corrections, a fresh, disjoint sample approved 38 of 40 and met the review criterion. Appendix~\ref{app:recent-human-review} gives the full review process and dispositions.

The current 100-input primary set and 131-input scorable set exclude every record with a retained negative or uncertain source, witness, or formalization review, and all three records with adverse judgments on their current line selections. Reapplying these exclusion lists leaves the current sets and scores unchanged. Appendix~\ref{app:recent-human-review} records the individual decisions; \path{bugbank/reviews/version_resolution/README.md} supplies the complete exclusion lists and saved-answer sensitivity. Reference-line revisions and their replay are documented in \path{benchmark/README.md}.

\subsection{Examining first-choice misses}
\label{app:ccl_miss_diagnostic}

A random sample of 30 was drawn from Sonnet 5's 58 readable first-choice misses in the earlier 191-input evaluation, using seed 20260921. LLM reviewers, with access to the source text and reference labels, classified the selected claims and assessed the accompanying explanations.

The final review identified 12 selections of another false assertion, 15 lines that were not false as written, and three source or annotation problems. The first LLM reviewer approved the supplied explanation in 11 of the 12 alternative-assertion cases. The three source or annotation problems were two omitted restatements of the challenged proposition and one garbled source.

The released records preserve the original classifications, later adjudications, sampling intervals, and disagreements on M08, M13, and M20. This analysis retained the original benchmark scores. The sampled responses and scientific reviews are in \path{bugbank/reviews/sonnet_misses/}; \path{bugbank/reviews/README.md} explains their numerical reproduction.

\end{filecontents*}

\begin{filecontents*}[overwrite]{fc__appendix__clear_metric_theory.tex}
\section{What the criticism scores measure}
\label{app:metrics}
This appendix distinguishes three questions relevant to interpreting the scores in Section~\ref{sec:evaluation}. They are whether a formal counterexample refutes the source claim, whether a critic selected a false assertion, and whether the critic's explanation is valid. The propositions are elementary, and we developed their written proofs with AI tools; they are separate from the Lean-certified mathematics elsewhere in the paper. The worked comparison uses the saved reviews of 24 texts from an earlier evaluation set.

\subsection{The logical connection from a formal witness to a source claim}
\label{app:transport}
Section~\ref{sec:evidence} defines the source assertion $Q_s$, the formal witness, and its interpretation $e$, and Equations~\eqref{eq:source-claim}--\eqref{eq:transport} state the source-correspondence requirements. The proposition below is the implication used there.

\begin{proposition}[One-sided refutation transport]
\label{prop:transport}
If Equation~\eqref{eq:formal-witness} and the witness-level implications in Equation~\eqref{eq:transport} hold, then $A_s(e(w))\wedge\neg C_s(e(w))$, so the source assertion $Q_s$ is false.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The first implication gives the source assumptions. If the source conclusion were true, the second implication would make the encoded conclusion true, contradicting the certificate. Hence the source conclusion is false at an allowed source object.
\end{proof}
Full equivalence between the formal and source statements is one way to satisfy these conditions, not the only one. The sorting example in Section~\ref{app:source-admissibility} illustrates the first condition: the witness must satisfy the source's endpoint inequality. Writing down $X_s,A_s,C_s$, and $e$ makes the interpretation a reviewer must judge explicit.

\subsection{Secondary reviews of generated objections}
\label{app:secondary-objections}
The Sonnet miss analysis in Section~\ref{app:ccl_miss_diagnostic} shows that a response can miss the designated reference line and still identify another false assertion. The study below checks, for each saved objection, whether it identifies its target and whether its evidence is valid.

\paragraph{Experiment B: LLM review of saved responses from an earlier evaluation set.} Experiment~B examined 48 saved responses, one from Gemini~3.1 Pro and one from Nemotron~3 Super 120B for each of 24 sampled texts of the earlier evaluation set. Two LLM reviewers separately read anonymized packets and judged whether each explanation challenged the selected claim (J1) and whether its mathematical evidence was valid (J3).

The question is whether a response both identifies the assertion it challenges and supplies valid evidence under that assertion's assumptions. Table~\ref{tab:b-results} reports this joint endpoint after the recorded LLM adjudications and the checks that each objection applies to the selected source claim under its assumptions; the first-reader judgments designated in advance are kept below. Six of the 10 reference misses supplied valid objections under the joint criterion.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{l c c c c}
\toprule
\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Reference} & \textbf{Valid obj.} & \textbf{Valid obj.} & \textbf{Total valid} \\
& \textbf{matches} & \textbf{among matches} & \textbf{among misses} & \textbf{objections} \\
\midrule
Gemini 3.1 Pro & 20 & 18 & 2 & 20 / 24 \\
Nemotron 3 Super 120B & 18 & 15 & 4 & 19 / 24 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}

A counted valid objection is a response that met the rules, challenges its selected claim, and supplies mathematically valid evidence under the source assumptions, after the recorded adjudications and the checks that the objection applies to the selected source claim. Table~\ref{tab:b-results-appendix} gives the first-reader judgments and the counts across the recorded review stages.}
\label{tab:b-results}
\end{table}

A third LLM reviewer examined eight flagged responses. P06 contained a complete explanation, although the attempt exceeded the allowed budget. P43 supplied valid mathematical evidence but cited the wrong line. P35 challenged a stronger condition than the selected statement claimed, so its counterexample did not address that claim. For P31, the calculation refuted the implication stated in the model's explanation, while the supplied example violated two assumptions in the source. Applying the study's rule that counterexamples must satisfy the source assumptions classifies P31's evidence as invalid.

\subsection{Partial labels and generated-objection soundness}
A reference-selection score measures agreement with the designated target. Two broader outcomes are finding any false assertion and supplying a valid counterexample for it. Because the reference labels cover selected assertions only and the score does not assess explanations, the available data bound these outcomes without determining them, a partial identification problem~\citep{manski2003partial}.

In this subsection $N$ is the number of benchmark inputs and all sums over $i$ run from one to $N$. For item $i$, let $F_i$ be the set of admissible line labels that locate genuinely false assertions under the source assumptions (a label may identify part of a displayed proposition); this set describes the truth of the assertions and need not be fully annotated. Let $G_i$ be the set of reference lines and $V_i$ the indicator that the response met the rules. For a first-choice label $\ell_i$, define
\[
h=\frac1N\sum_i V_i\ind\{\ell_i\in G_i\},\quad
q=\frac1N\sum_i V_i\ind\{\ell_i\in F_i\},\quad
c=\frac1N\sum_i V_i.
\]
The fractions $h$ and $q$ count responses that meet the rules and select, respectively, a reference line or any genuinely false assertion; $c$ counts all responses that meet the rules. All three use the full $N$-input denominator. Unlike $h$ and $c$, $q$ is not observable in general, because the full sets $F_i$ are not annotated. Null labels belong to neither set.
\begin{proposition}[Partial identification under sound references]
\label{prop:partial}
If $G_i\subseteq F_i$ for every item, then $h\le q\le c$. If the inclusion may fail on at most $m$ items, then
\[
\max(0,h-m/N)\le q\le c.
\]
Let $E_i$ indicate that the critic's supplied objection is valid for its selected target, and define $r=N^{-1}\sum_i V_i\ind\{\ell_i\in F_i\}E_i$. Without evidence about $E_i$, no strictly positive lower bound on $r$ follows from $h$ alone.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
On a sound item, reference membership implies false-assertion membership. At most $m$ reference hits can fail that implication on the remaining items. No valid target can be credited without a valid output, giving the upper bound $c$. Finally, every $E_i$ can be zero even when every chosen target matches its reference set: a critic may attach an invalid witness to the correct line. Hence $r=0$ is compatible with positive or perfect reference localization.
\end{proof}
The same argument applies to top-$k$ selection, with explanation validity assessed separately. When the designated references are sound, matching one is evidence that the critic selected a false assertion, and a miss can still identify a different false assertion, as the diagnostic in Section~\ref{app:ccl_miss_diagnostic} shows; estimating $q$ or $r$ requires the corresponding target and explanation judgments.

\subsection{Comparing critics with partially reviewed line selections}
\label{app:sharp-comparison}
If two critics select the same line, its truth status affects both scores equally, so only the selections on which they disagree can change their score difference. The following result turns that observation into exact bounds.

Set a critic's first-choice label to $\bot$ when its response does not meet the rules or its first-choice label is invalid, so that critic $a$ or $b$ supplies $p_i^a,p_i^b\in L_i\cup\{\bot\}$, where $L_i$ is the addressable line set. Write $z_{i\ell}=\ind\{\ell\in F_i\}$ and $z_{i\bot}=0$. A line reviewed as false under the source assumptions has value one; a line reviewed as not false has value zero. Every other indicator is unknown. A reference label has value one only when its validity under the source assumptions is assumed or established.

For critic $m\in\{a,b\}$, let $\underline z_i^m,\overline z_i^m$ be the lower and upper possible values of $z_{i,p_i^m}$. They are $(1,1)$ for known false assertions, $(0,0)$ for known nonfalse or null predictions, and $(0,1)$ for unadjudicated lines. Indicators of the same line on the same item are shared, not assigned separately to each critic.

\begin{proposition}[Sharp comparison in the line-label model]
\label{prop:sharp-comparison}
Over assignments of unknown line indicators consistent with these known labels and with no further constraints, the false-assertion localization difference
\[
\Delta_F=\frac1N\sum_i\bigl(z_{i,p_i^a}-z_{i,p_i^b}\bigr)
\]
has sharp lower and upper bounds
\begin{align}
L&=\frac1N\sum_{i:p_i^a\ne p_i^b}(\underline z_i^a-\overline z_i^b),&
U&=\frac1N\sum_{i:p_i^a\ne p_i^b}(\overline z_i^a-\underline z_i^b).
\label{eq:sharp-comparison}
\end{align}
Both endpoints are attained in this model. ``Sharp'' means that no narrower interval follows from these labels alone under the stated absence of further constraints.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Coincident predictions share an indicator, so their contribution is exactly zero even when it is unknown. For distinct predictions the difference is at least $\underline z_i^a-\overline z_i^b$ and at most $\overline z_i^a-\underline z_i^b$. To attain the lower bound, set each unknown indicator selected by $a$ to zero and each selected by $b$ to one on disagreement items; reverse these assignments for the upper bound. Distinct selected lines impose no conflicting assignment within an item, and this model imposes no cross-item constraints. Thus the extremal choices can be made simultaneously.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Which additional reviews narrow the comparison.}
The identification width is
\[
U-L=\frac1N\sum_{i:p_i^a\ne p_i^b}
\bigl[(\overline z_i^a-\underline z_i^a)+(\overline z_i^b-\underline z_i^b)\bigr].
\]
Only unknown predictions on disagreement items contribute: one unknown line gives width $1/N$, two give $2/N$, and a shared unknown prediction gives zero. Reviewing one such line narrows the interval whichever label it receives, so the disagreements identify the additional source judgments that matter most for the paired comparison.

The bounds are exact for the stated model, which permits every assignment consistent with the known line labels; logical relationships among assertions or repeated sources can narrow them further. An identification interval describes the possible score differences while some labels are unknown, whereas a sampling confidence interval describes uncertainty from sampling texts, and reviewing explanation validity answers a third question, whether the critic supplies valid evidence for its chosen assertion. Applying the bounds requires item-level predictions and judgments.

\paragraph{Numerical application on 24 texts.} The proposition concerns which selected assertions are false, not whether the critic's own explanation proves them false. Using the saved judgments and source checks, we assign one shared truth indicator to each $(\text{input}, \text{line})$ pair: a valid objection can establish a positive line label, while an invalid explanation alone does not establish a negative one.

Gemini and Nemotron make the same normalized prediction on 15 of 24 inputs; their contribution is exactly zero regardless of the line's status. They disagree on nine inputs. Let $z_{19}$ and $z_{43}$ be the unknown false-assertion indicators for Nemotron's P19 line 24 and P43 line 230. The seven other disagreement contributions sum to zero based on the accepted labels or null predictions. These two contribute $-z_{19}$ and $1 - z_{43}$. Thus the Gemini-minus-Nemotron score difference is $(1 - z_{19} - z_{43})/24$, between $-1/24$ and $1/24$. 

Positive line labels are accepted conditionally from the saved LLM judgments, all other non-null labels stay unknown, the references are not assumed sound, and no negative label is inferred from invalid evidence. These are the 24 paired texts of Experiment B, separate from the current 100-input benchmark. The interval $[-1/24,1/24]$ is conditional on the recorded LLM judgments and summarizes the two unknown labels; it is not a 95\% confidence interval. The saved inputs, judgments, and reproducible calculation are documented in \path{studies/criticism_endpoints/README.md}.

\subsection{Sensitivity to revised reference lines}
Let $k$ be the number of candidate slots scored. Fix $P_i^k$, the set of valid line labels in the original first $k$ slots for input $i$; null slots remain unfilled and later predictions are not promoted. Write $G_i\triangle G_i' $ for labels that belong to exactly one of the old and new reference sets. Let $G_i,G'_i$ be old and revised reference sets and $r=|\{i:G_i\ne G'_i\}|$.
\begin{proposition}[Prediction-aware reference perturbation]
\label{prop:perturb}
For fixed outputs and a fixed $N$-item population,
\begin{align}
\left|\Hit_G@k-\Hit_{G'}@k\right|
&\le \rho(k):=\frac1N\sum_i
\ind\{P_i^k\cap(G_i\triangle G'_i)\ne\varnothing\}
\le\frac rN.
\label{eq:prediction-aware-revision}
\end{align}
For two critics $a,b$, their paired score difference changes by at most $\rho_a(k)+\rho_b(k)\le2r/N$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
An item cannot change hit status unless a selected label lies in the symmetric difference $G_i\triangle G'_i$. Even then, a retained reference hit may keep its status unchanged. Each binary hit changes by at most one, so summing gives the first inequality. Only items whose reference set changed can contribute to $\rho$, giving the second. Applying the triangle inequality to two critics yields the paired bound. The coarse constants are attainable: a changed reference can turn one miss into a hit, or exchange reference membership between two distinct first-choice predictions, changing their difference by two.
\end{proof}
The prediction-aware bound counts outcomes that could change, not net changes: a gained hit and a lost hit can cancel in an aggregate score, and an edit away from every selected line cannot affect the score at all. Counting changed reference sets alone can therefore overstate the possible effect of a revision.

The bound applies to any recorded revision of the checked line sets, and rescoring the saved predictions against both versions gives the actual change. If $m$ excluded inputs are misses for every reporting view, each rate changes from $H/N$ to $H/(N-m)$ while the hit count $H$ stays fixed. The release provides the line sets, exclusion lists, and per-input predictions for these calculations.

\subsection{Why paired comparisons require individual outcomes}
Suppose two models have $a$ and $b$ hits on the same $N$ items, with $a\ge b$. Their common-hit count $t$ can lie anywhere in
\[
\max(0,a+b-N)\le t\le b.
\]
The discordant counts are $a-t$ and $b-t$, so a paired test needs more than the marginals. In the current 100-input results, Gemini has 73 first-choice matches and Opus 68; these totals allow $t$ anywhere from 41 to 68, that is, between 5 and 59 inputs on which exactly one model succeeds. The released per-input predictions determine the actual overlap. The main text therefore reports the highest observed scores, and paired comparisons use the item-level outcomes.

\subsection{Costs with incomplete usage records}
Let the total nonnegative costs in two conditions be $C_A=L_A+U_A$ and $C_B=L_B+U_B$, where $L_A,L_B$ are recorded subtotals and $U_A,U_B\ge0$ unrecorded usage. For positive denominators, $L_A/L_B$ bounds $C_A/C_B$ in neither direction in general, because either unrecorded remainder can be the larger one. A budget charge is an accounting debit, not a measurement of that remainder, and a cost total over selected successful attempts describes a different set of executions from one that includes failures and retries. We therefore name the included calls and report measured usage, lower bounds, and budget charges separately.
\end{filecontents*}
\begin{filecontents*}[overwrite]{fc__appendix__corpus_audit_procedures.tex}
\section{Corpus construction, audit procedures, and executable checks}
\label{sec:method}

\paragraph{Workflow and recorded search limits.}
This appendix gives the procedures behind the six-stage workflow of Section~\ref{sec:resource}: the search limits, the prompts' requirements, the independent checks, and the model review. The original auditing protocol favors short objects: at most 12 graph vertices, 10 objects, or 10 significant trace steps. For each research conjecture we separately specify the types of examples to examine and the ranges of parameter values to test. A separately written checking program tests each proposed witness with exact arithmetic. Human contributors and coding agents translate surviving failures into Lean and inspect the selected theorem and its proof dependencies. A separate model-assisted review judges whether the objection applies to the selected source claim and records what it shows about the surrounding argument. The stages follow the principle of producing a result and checking its certificate separately \citep{mcconnell2011certifying,moura2021lean,mathlib2020}.

A successful exhaustive check establishes the claim only for the example types and sizes it covers, and separately written checking programs and separate model calls can share mistaken assumptions. Appendix~\ref{app:recent-human-review} documents the human reviews and the human-approved core that supplies the 100 primary benchmark inputs.

\subsection{Generating and checking counterexamples}
\label{sec:method-generation}

Let $P$ be an informal proof, $q$ a claim used in $P$, and $w$ a proposed counterexample. Checking the objection requires three things: $w$ must violate the stated mathematical condition, that condition must represent $q$ under the source's assumptions, and the checking program must perform the intended calculation. The workflow checks the three separately and saves the evidence from each stage.

\paragraph{What each check establishes.}
A separately written program tests the proposed witness, and a Lean proof certifies the formal counterexample~\citep{moura2021lean,mathlib2020}; file hashes, source-passage matching, and axiom checks link that proof to its dossier. Human review, where performed, checks whether the objection represents the source claim and satisfies its assumptions. An executable Python check also depends on the Python interpreter. The division follows certifying algorithms and proof-carrying code: a producer supplies evidence that a smaller checker can verify~\citep{mcconnell2011certifying,necula1997pcc}.

\paragraph{Selecting claims to investigate.}
Two separate calls to the same fixed model version receive the problem and a line-numbered proof, and each identifies a claim that may admit a short counterexample, within the size limits above and with a target Lean proof of roughly 50 lines. The response must quote the source exactly, describe a concrete candidate, and supply the required metadata. A deterministic validator checks the quotation and the response format, and a second model then reviews the claim, its assumptions, the relevance of the quotation, and the proposed size. These calls only choose candidates: the programs and Lean proofs that follow test the witnesses, and separate reviews check their connection to the source.

\paragraph{Searching, checking, and proving.}
A search agent receives the record identifier, the quoted claim, and the surrounding text, but not earlier witnesses or Lean files, so that it attempts a fresh construction. It writes and runs a deterministic program over integers and exact rationals, and records one of four outcomes: a witness, an exhaustive search with no witness in a specified finite set, an inconclusive search, or a claim outside the permitted search setting. A separately written checker reconstructs the mathematical condition from the claim and tests the proposed witness; both programs are replayed with two hash seeds. Human contributors and coding agents translated suitable witnesses into Lean~4 and built them with a fixed toolchain. The audit enumerates each theorem's axiom dependencies with \texttt{Lean.collectAxioms}, distinguishes computation trusted to the compiler from kernel-checked proofs, and detects \texttt{sorryAx}; every selected declaration in the current release has a kernel-checked proof. Separate stage inputs and fresh processes keep each stage's evidence inspectable.

\paragraph{Connecting files to claims.}
The record stores SHA-256 hashes of the problem, solution, quoted claim, certificate, and original source passage; when matching uses normalized text, an index map identifies the original bytes. A scanner checks commands, declarations, and imports against the allowed configuration. The reporting code is checked separately from the Lean kernel~\citep{wiedijk2012pollack}, with an attack suite that exercises wrong targets, added axioms, \texttt{sorry}, altered source passages, imports, duplicate rows, aliases, and aggregate counts; the saved results distinguish completed tests from tests not run.

\subsection{Reviewing the recorded objection}
\label{sec:method-revalidation}

The model review assigns three complementary checks to fresh agents. A blind input contains the problem and the quoted claim; a full input adds the witness, explanation, and certificate. Review documents are assembled from these files, with hashes recomputed from disk and each quotation located in the numbered source text.

\paragraph{Three mathematical checks.}
The first agent uses only the blind input to seek a new counterexample
with exact arithmetic, recording its finite search set and whether the
search is exhaustive. The second reads the full input, writes a checker
without seeing the first agent's work, and substitutes the recorded
witness values. It reports whether the witness works and identifies any
information needed to reproduce it. The third compares the Lean
certificate with the quoted claim and witness: do the definitions encode
the source's objects and conditions, does the construction use the
recorded values, and does the theorem refute the intended assertion?
Any partial match must name the modeling choice or discrepancy.

\paragraph{Resolving disagreements.}
A fourth model agent examines flagged records, the three verdicts, and the generated programs. It checks the disputed mathematics with its own exact-arithmetic program and reads the quotation in context. Its conclusion distinguishes an error in the record or checker from a misplaced quotation or a documented modeling choice, and it recommends keeping, repairing, removing, or referring the record for human review. An accepted adjudication includes an executable check and a mathematical justification. The saved decisions identify unresolved disputes and required repairs. Records pass after all checks succeed or an adjudication resolves every flag without requiring a change. A second model campaign repeated these checks on the admitted inputs with the same prompts and fresh processes; its verdicts were saved separately, and the intersecting passing sets supplied the initial human-review handout.

\paragraph{Human review and reproduction.}
Human-review packets include the source context, the selected theorem, the complete Lean file, and its project-local dependencies; Appendix~\ref{app:recent-human-review} presents the review process, and Section~\ref{app:human-verification} the initial review conditions and version evidence. In the repository, \path{bugbank/reviews/README.md} indexes the completed human judgments and \path{bugbank/core/README.md} explains current admission; the saved model-audit decisions and construction lineage are available through \path{bugbank/annotations/README.md} and \path{bugbank/annotations/initial401/results/audit_analysis.json}.
\end{filecontents*}
\begin{filecontents*}[overwrite]{fc__appendix__historical_corpus_audit.tex}
\subsection{Executable checks of the proposed witnesses}
\label{sec:results}




\label{sec:results-twins}

Across 604 executable tasks, the search produced 351 witnesses, 12 exhaustive searches without a witness in the specified finite set, 221 inconclusive searches, and 20 claims outside the permitted search setting. Separately written checkers confirmed 348 of the 351 proposed witnesses and rejected three because they did not violate the reconstructed condition, and all 955 search and checker programs passed exact-arithmetic inspection and deterministic replay with two hash seeds. A separate stage checked 16 selected algorithmic failures and confirmed all 16. These were chosen examples, so the result describes their checks rather than the fraction of all failures that the search would find. The repository supplies the outcomes and a count-replay command in \path{studies/executable_checks/README.md}, with program bindings and the 16 selected records in \path{studies/executable_checks/bindings/README.md}.
\end{filecontents*}
